IVCA raises alarm over foreign VC dominating Irish SME rounds
2026-09-13

IVCA raises alarm over foreign VC dominating Irish SME rounds

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-13

Visit Original Website
‘These levels are flashing red warning signals’, says IVCA chairperson Richard Watson. The Irish Venture Capital Association (IVCA) has raised concerns over the influence of international venture capital funding in Irish domestic companies, as foreign contributions represented 82pc of the total investment in Irish SMEs in the first half of 2026. “These levels are flashing red warning signals as they represent some of the highest on record and emphasise our over-exposure to overseas investors,” said IVCA chairperson Richard Watson of the VenturePulse survey data published today (13 September). Global geopolitical instability, including the war in Iran, as well as the significant concentration of US capital in blockbuster AI deals could well impact future international VC investment into Ireland, he said. Turbulent geopolitical conditions caused a 71pc fall-off in international investment in Q4 last year, leading to a 46pc decline in total funding for the quarter, while contributing to a 23pc drop-off in funding for the year. The “rollercoaster” year for funding was attributed to an “undoubted Trump effect”, after uncertainty caused by tariffs led to the worst second quarter for 10 years. Meanwhile, VC funding of AI giants has dominated the global charts in recent years, led by Anthropic and OpenAI – two of the biggest private companies in the world that between them took 43pc of global start-up funding in the first half of 2026. Total venture capital funding into Irish SMEs fell 10pc to €578.4m in the first half of 2026, while the second quarter marked year-on-year growth of more than 200pc to €356.7m. That significant jump, however, is in comparison to the same period last year – which was the worst in 10 years. Of the more than half-a-billion euros in funding raised by Irish SMEs in the first half of this year, deals of more than €30m grew by 3pc, while all funding brackets under €5m dropped. Comparatively, in the second quarter, deals crossing €30m grew by a considerable 570pc, while there were no deals in the €10m to €30m range. Two companies – Dublin fintech provider Fonoa and Belfast cybersecurity firm Cloudsmith – accounted for nearly 44pc of the total raised in the second quarter, led majorly by American venture firms. Other major rounds in Q2 included drone delivery company Manna, which raised €43m; fleet safety tech provider CameraMatics, which raised €49m; and AI space-tech Ubotica. Smaller deals included women’s health-tech Peri’s €100,000 round and AI accessibility start-up Nexus Inclusion’s €1.5m raise. The data highlights the “extent to which the dearth of domestic sources of funding is limiting capital formation”, said IVCA director-general Sarah-Jane Larkin. Life sciences and fintech companies led the way in the first half of the year, both accounting for 26pc of total funding, followed by cybersecurity and software at 14pc each. Meanwhile, AI and machine learning represented 7pc. “This does not mean we are being left behind in AI,” commented Larkin. “It is unrealistic to expect a small nation like Ireland to produce the giant platform and frontier AI leaders of the US. But AI is now embedded in the products and services of the vast majority of local VC-backed SMEs.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. More than half of AI job postings seek skills beyond job titles, research finds
2026-09-11

More than half of AI job postings seek skills beyond job titles, research finds

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
Andela collected data from 47,101 technical job postings from Fortune 500 companies and 2,026 skill descriptions. New research from global talent platform Andela has found that in a working landscape being transformed daily by AI, organisations are failing to update their job descriptions and titles in line with changes. Andela’s ‘Emerging Skills Research’ collected data from 47,101 technical job postings from Fortune 500 companies and 2,026 skill descriptions. A core finding of the research was the revelation that 53pc of AI job postings are asking for skills that don’t match the listed job title, highlighting a disconnect between organisations, recruiters and jobseekers. “AI is changing tech workflows faster than ever and we need new ways to get ahead of those changes,” said Carrol Chang, Andela’s CEO. “This groundbreaking research, based on our proprietary skills taxonomy updated for the AI world, provides a headlight, not a rear-view mirror. “With it, companies will identify emerging roles and skills ahead of the market, enabling both enterprises and employees to be more strategic and successful.” Andela’s research found that among the roughly 1,832 postings targeting people with titles such as AI engineer and ML engineer, more than half of the required skills pulled from at least two different and established roles. The report said: “For familiar titles like AI engineer, companies are seeking a different, unnamed role that includes skills such as LLM orchestration, autonomous agent and vector databases. Or, companies hire data scientists but then ask them to ship LLM agents.” Reviewing job listings from Fortune 500 companies, Andela found that 23 ‘groups’ of skills kept appearing together, but without any official job title being attached. Eight of the groups appeared to suggest a brand new kind of job, 14 were found to be hybrids of old jobs with new skill expectations, and one was dropped due to a lack of confirmed data. Two examples of ‘new jobs’ discovered are MLOps pipeline engineer, which combines parts of five existing job roles into one, and LLM application engineer, where someone builds apps using AI models that already exist, instead of building those models from scratch. Andela found 6,758 job listings essentially asking for an LLM application engineer – they listed all the relevant skills, but the companies didn’t use that job title because it doesn’t officially exist yet. This shows companies need these skills faster than the job market has caught up with proper names for them, according to the research. The report said: “The findings underscore the challenges companies face, driven by the unprecedented speed of change in the post-AI era, to accurately identify emerging skills, name them and attach them to job roles. Mismatches result in lost time, money and opportunity for both employer and employee. “Job descriptions written for yesterday’s roles filter out the candidates companies actually need. Companies that fail to see emerging needs for human skills, especially as AI changes what it can do, will continually chase the wrong thing, and companies who hire today for yesterday’s roles will likely accrue ‘talent debt’ alongside technical debt.” According to Cory Hymel, an author on the report and Andela’s head of research, gaps that widen the divide between what companies think they are hiring for and what the role truly requires are becoming structural liabilities. As of now, there are a number of emerging tech roles as identified in the research, which include: MLOps pipeline engineer; LLM application engineer; FinOps reliability engineer; docs-as-code engineer; product front-end engineer; lakehouse analytics engineer, DevSecOps security engineer; and SecOps observability engineer. Chinese Government Dismisses Tech Billionaire Calls for an AI Slowdown as “Fear-Mongering”

We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. In a whiplash-inducing turn away from the mythical quest to build human-level artificial intelligence, Western tech billionaires like Dario Amodei, Sam Altman, and Elon Musk are now joining hands to call for a massive slowdown of the tech’s development. The commentariat is divided on what exactly this means — for some, it’s proof-positive of a genuine threat that AI could spin out of control and exterminate everybody. To others it’s a smokescreen, a thin cover provided by AI CEOs who are desperate for a government bailout to avoid popping the AI investment bubble. If you ask lawmakers in Beijing, the whole thing is a bunch of nonsense. In a routine press scrum hosted by China’s Ministry of Foreign Affairs, government spokesperson Guo Jiakun addressed a laundry list of topics, from President Xi Jinping’s recent trip to New Delhi to recovery efforts around the devastating mudslides in Nepal. One of the questions, posed by a Reuters journalist, concerned whether the ministry had any comment on calls from American tech billionaires to slow down AI development. “AI is a consequential technology for the wellbeing of all humanity,” Guo replied. “All parties should jointly promote the open and inclusive development of AI for good and for all. Fear-mongering, confrontation and vicious competition will only hamper efforts toward sound global AI governance, which serves no one’s interest.” Compared to the weight given to other topics, the ministry’s statement on the Western AI debacle was decidedly terse. While talk of an AI slowdown is roiling US markets and trending on social media, it seems like it’s barely a blip on Beijing’s radar. The virtual hand-waving follows the decidedly disinterested stance Chinese policymakers have taken toward the US approach to AI development, which for years has hinged on building toward human-level artificial general intelligence. As research fellow at the partially US-funded Brookings Institute Kyle Chan wrote in February, Beijing’s concept of “AGI” is wildly different from the one used across the pond. While US policymakers and industry leaders treat the tech as a race to AGI where one winner takes all, their Chinese counterparts aren’t in any rush. The US might get there first, the idea goes, but like electric vehicles, cancer treatments, and smartphones, Chinese industry can quickly make up the difference. “Let’s look at the history of the internet. It started in the US, but China quickly caught up and led in consumer applications — WeChat is a world-class example,” professor emeritus at the Hong Kong University of Science and Technology Yang Qiang said during a panel at the AGI Next Summit in January. “AI is similar — it’s a tool, and Chinese ingenuity will push it to the limit.” More on China and AI: Top Chinese Court Issues Sweeping Legal Crackdown on AI Deepfakes

Amazon Has Become Pathetically Reliant on Food Stamps to Power Its Empire

Futurism | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. After over two decades of operation, Amazon Prime has become the backbone of American commerce, more akin to a public utility than a private company. Its logistics network has blown past the US Postal Service in parcel volume, delivering crucial goods to small business owners, trade workers, and teachers at record speed. And like any public utility, Amazon Prime comes with a public price tag — a massive state subsidy paid by taxpayers around the country. Blistering new reporting by Fortune revealed that the number of Amazon workers receiving government assistance via Supplemental Nutrition Assistance Program (SNAP) and Medicaid has nearly tripled between 2020 and 2025, a stunning rise for any group, let alone workers employed at one of the wealthiest companies in the world. It comes as the megacorporation posted a massive rise in annual profit over the same period, from $11.6 billion in 2020 to $77.7 billion in 2025. Those federal assistance numbers, obtained by the independent US Government Accountability Office, found that 12,346 Amazon workers across 11 states were getting help affording basic groceries through SNAP. A further 11,338 in those states rely on Medicaid. The federal assistant amounts to a de facto subsidy of Amazon’s labor force, allowing the company executives to rake in record profits while paying its workers starvation wages. If it weren’t for these government programs, in other words, thousands of Amazon’s lowest-paid workers wouldn’t be able to afford to make ends meet. Though Amazon was far from the only company whose full time employees are struggling, the GAO survey found that Amazon was consistently among the very top employers of adult SNAP and Medicaid enrollees. Other top offenders include Walmart, ride-share platform companies like Uber, and chain stores like McDonald’s and Dollar General. Beyond Amazon, the survey observes the grim reality of working class life in the wealthiest nation on Earth. Overall, the report points to the fact that nearly 14 million working adults were enrolled in Medicaid in 2024, while 10.6 million received SNAP benefits. Of those receiving government aid for groceries, some 71.3 percent — about 7.5 million people — worked 35 hours or more per week. “What this analysis really points to is the large number of people who have very low incomes and continue to have very low income,” GAO director for education, workforce, and income security issues Kathryn Larin told Fortune. “I mean, these are families that are really barely able to make ends meet, and yet they are working, and they are working a lot.” More on Amazon: Amazon Investigating Its Own Employees for Daring to Question AI Data Centers

Remember how Elon Musk vowed that his AI Grok would make an anti-woke, “historically accurate” version of the Odyssey? Well, he ended up throwing together a whole contest around it — and the winner of the first place prize of $100,000 is, as you probably expected, laughable nonsense. The problems run deep. The Cyclops Polyphemus is depicted with three eyes, which feels disqualifying. And just a few cuts later, the famously monocular giant is at least four times as large as he was before. The other creatures and gods look like something out of a mobile game; Poseidon emerges from the sea appearing conspicuously cartoonish and impossibly muscled, before smashing Odysseus’s ship and his crew of photorealistic, ripped men. Half the dialog is delivered in dramatic whispers with the same cadence. The fight scenes are cut to disguise the actual moments of impact, in the same way real B-movies do with aging action stars. There are some shots here and there that look convincing enough, if you can overlook their bland aesthetic. But this AI “film” suffers the same tragic flaw that all AI films do: their component clips fail to coalesce into a coherent whole. It doesn’t do much on the “historically accurate” front, either; some of the Greek soldiers are clearly wearing the armor donned by Roman legionnaires. Nonetheless, its creator Nem Perez declared that “this is ‘The Odyssey’ as Homer intended,” before calling it his “most intricate work to date.” He also labeled the video, in oxymoronic fashion, a “5-minute epic.” This is "The Odyssey" as Homer intended. My longest, most intricate work to date. I pushed @grok @imagine to its absolute limits to create this 5-minute epic. What bothered me most about the recent adaptation was the omission of the Phaeacians and Princess Nausicaa, whom… https://t.co/awYW6Hu4W8 pic.twitter.com/hJHOgdkEec It’s unclear if Musk actually watched the winner; as far as we can tell, he hasn’t replied or retweeted it, something he’s done for other AI Odyssey adaptations. Nolan, Musk accused, “desecrated Homer and groveled on his knees just to meet the woke rules required to win an Oscar.” As such, he vowed that his AI “Grok Imagine will make a full-length movie of The Odyssey that is historically accurate and true to the art of Homer.” We’re still waiting for that full-length adaptation. But the short films that the contest has produced already make it painfully clear that AI video generators, and the “filmmakers” that embrace them, simply aren’t up to snuff. In all of the top three entries, Odysseus looks more or less like the same guy — some weird, looksmaxxer’s fantasy of a buff Antonio Banderas. Is this a fault of Grok or Grok users? Anthropic Boasts It Would Be Profitable if You Ignore How Much It Costs to Develop AI

Futurism | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. Despite being one of the world leaders in AI development, the private firm Anthropic has yet to post a quarterly profit. That operating deficit isn’t for a lack of trying — and, as the company tells it, could be reversing very soon, at least if you’re willing to swallow one massive caveat. According to new reporting by the Financial Times, the company behind the Claude told a select group of trusted investors that its adjusted operating income (AOI) is set to be positive for the second consecutive quarter. AOI is an accounting technique that strips out non-recurring costs, giving backers a very specific look under a company’s hood. Posting a profit on AOI is very different from posting a profit on net income, otherwise known as the “bottom line,” under which Anthropic remains billions of dollars in the red. Aside from the fact that Anthropic is not about to turn a real profit anytime soon, it may still be a noteworthy signal. The AI world is currently awash in fear-mongering over AI safety, a phenomena some analysts say could signal a turn away from the race-to-the-bottom to build artificial general intelligence toward a more reasonable — if less ambitious — embrace of AI as it currently is. While it remains to be seen how these narratives and business realities play out in practice, if the top American AI companies choose to stop and let the dust settle, Anthropic may very well find itself ahead of its competition. Though Anthropic is still far underwater, companies like ChatGPT developer OpenAI are somehow even deeper. As lead AI analyst at the firm SemiAnalysis Joey Brookhart told the FT, “if you continue to operate at these margins and growth rates, it will be so hard to compete [with Anthropic] because they have so much [in the way of computing resources].” Still, there are some questions in the AI lab’s story that have yet to be answered. For one thing, this profitability forecast — a flattering projection conveniently leaked to the financial press at a pivotal moment — comes in place of a highly anticipated public prospectus which the company was expected to make public sometime last week. Anthropic may still be planning to release the prospectus soon, but it’s worth noting the company has chosen to lead with spectacle and innuendo, as opposed to the formal legal document. For months, Anthropic has been teasing a public stock offering that hasn’t yet materialized, and it remains to be seen whether the latest slowdown talk will impact that timeline. In fact, there’s reason to believe this song and dance is all part of the lead-up to the AI lab’s IPO, as investors whip themselves into a frenzy about AI safety. As Anthropic investor Brad Gerstner said in a post on X-formerly-Twitter over the weekend, “Anthropic will IPO” anyway, because there is “huge appetite to invest in the AI leaders,” especially those which are seen as bringing “even more transparency, scrutiny, accountability” into the AI space (at least as far as Gerstner’s investments are concerned.) While the US appears to be further away from meaningful AI regulations than ever before, lawmakers certainly have plenty of ideas on how the country could go about setting meaningful rules. It’s a particularly precarious moment as fears over rogue AI models wreaking havoc and endangering humanity continue to mount. Even tech leaders from the majority of frontier AI labs have started to coalesce around the idea that the industry has to slow down the development of the tech to get ahead of a possible catastrophe. Vermont senator Bernie Sanders (VT-D) is intensifying his calls for a ban on the development of so-called superintelligent AIs — and he’s willing to go a lot further than some of his like-minded peers. “Dario Amodei, Elon Musk and Sam Altman now agree that we must slow down the development of AI and ‘pace the frontier,'” he tweeted last week. “That’s a start, but it’s not enough.” Sanders was referring to a lengthy essay titled “We Must Pace the Frontier,” in which Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei argued that the industry should open its doors to third-party evaluators, establish common safety standards, and coordinate with other democracies around the world to enforce these standards. “When you are racing towards a cliff, you don’t just ease up on the gas pedal,” Sanders added. “You hit the brakes.” This month, the lawmaker announced the “Ban Artificial Superintelligence Act,” which is legislation designed to “stop AI oligarchs from building machines humans cannot control.” The bill, if enacted, would stop AI development “until a federal regulator has established safety rules.” It would also set new penalties for anybody attempting to circumvent these rules. AI developers who try to build superintelligent AI anyway could be “subject to not more than 20 years in prison, which is similar to existing penalties related to unlawfully developing nuclear weapons.” Sanders has come up with plenty of other radical ideas to stop the AI industry from concentrating immense amounts of wealth. In June, he proposed creating a “one-time 50 percent tax” on the stock of AI companies, and transferring the money into a public fund to be managed by the government. Lawmakers became particularly concerned after a group of OpenAI AI models hacked the systems of Hugging Face, a pivotal cybersecurity failing that heightened calls to slow the development of AI. A Senate subcommittee has since announced that it’s looking into OpenAI and its response to the incident, as Axios reported last week. It’s a strange moment in time. While Sanders continues to threaten AI CEOs with regulatory action, those same executives appear open to having the government set the rules. But whether lawmakers will ever be able to find consensus remains unclear. The executive branch, for one, has demonstrated little interest in an AI slowdown. Earlier today, president Donald Trump mocked the very concept in a Truth Social post on Monday, arguing that there was a “SICK conspiracy going on against AI and Data Centers” without offering any evidence. The Planet Mercury Appears to Have Shrunken Drastically, “Like a Balloon Left Out in the Cold”
2026-09-14

The Planet Mercury Appears to Have Shrunken Drastically, “Like a Balloon Left Out in the Cold”

Futurism | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. Scientists say that the planet Mercury has been actively shrinking over the billions of years that it’s orbited the Sun, a process that’s leaving cracked-up wrinkles behind like an old apple. At just one-third the size of Earth, it’s already the smallest planet in our solar system — it’s larger than Pluto, but Pluto is no longer classified as a planet — in a title it’s bound to hold on to as its continues to diminish in physical size. Now, as detailed in a new paper by a team of scientists from the German Aerospace Center’s (DLR) Institute of Space Research, the oddball planet’s shrivelling could be occurring at an even faster-than-expected rate, losing up to 14.5 miles of total diameter since it formed billions of years ago. That’s anywhere from ten to 30 percent more than previously thought. The scientists suggest that debris from impact craters may have hidden this accelerated shrinking, a process largely thought to be the result of Mercury running out of heat — “like a balloon left out in the cold,” per an accompanying press release. The findings could help scientists get a better sense of what’s going on inside the planet and how it evolved over time. “More shrinking means Mercury could have a larger metal core, less light elements like silicon mixed into the metal core, or a higher starting temperature,” DLR planetary scientist Gaku Nishiyama explained in a statement. “Every planet is an odd little duck that has a story to tell,” Brown University planetary geoscientist Sam Birch, who was not involved in the study, told the New York Times. “If you can understand how fast Mercury is shrinking, you can understand how fast it’s cooling, what size it started at and what it’s made out of.” Mercury’s outermost layer of hard rock has been resisting — or at least trying to resist — this deflation, causing its surface to crack and “wrinkle.” It’s a rough surface lined with steep cliffs known as scarps, as well as ridges that make getting a broader sense of how much it’s shrinking overall difficult. By aggregating previous maps of the planet’s surface, the team found evidence that the roughest areas have fewer wrinkles, suggesting that impact debris is “obscuring shortening structures,” according to Nishiyama. Their newly revised rates of shrinking also happen to better reflect previous physics-based predictions. Fortunately, we’re about to have a close look at the deflating planet. The European Space Agency and Japan Aerospace Exploration Agency’s BepiColombo mission, which launched in 2018, will enter Mercury’s orbit in November, if all goes according to plan — becoming only the second spacecraft to do so in human history. Cornelis is part of a new wave of AI infrastructure companies emerging to break apart Nvidia’s market dominance, piece by piece (or chip by chip, one could say). Amazon Prime Video takes on TikTok with short-form news clips

TechCrunch | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt Amazon Prime Video is coming after TikTok with the addition of short-form news clips. The company said on Monday that it’s expanding its news coverage to include on-demand news clips offering both local and national stories. These will be available on TVs and other living room devices, but will soon roll out to the web and Prime Video’s mobile apps, the company said. The clips will be available in the News destination within the Prime Video app in its Trending Topics and the Local News carousels. They join Prime Video’s existing local, national, and world news coverage from a number of major providers, which are available to everyone in the U.S. regardless of whether they have a Prime subscription. Amazon could not yet confirm whether the clips on mobile would be oriented for vertical video or not, only that they would be considered short-form content on iOS and Android. While TikTok is better known as an entertainment destination, it’s also, along with Instagram, increasingly a place where younger people get their news. Gen Z in particular often watches news creators and influencers on short-form apps, including YouTube Shorts. Amazon, which doesn’t have a true social destination of its own, is instead adding short-form video to Prime Video, in hopes of capturing Gen Z’s attention. But Gen Z viewers are drawn to these social apps not necessarily because of the length of the clips, but rather because of how the information is presented. Based on findings from Pew Research, these younger news consumers want short-form news content that “feels organic, not over-produced.” Prime Video is not the first streamer to add short-form clips to its content lineup. Peacock added support for short-form clips in March, followed by HBO Max and Netflix in July, then Disney+ in August. Combined, the updates represent an industrywide effort to meet viewers where they are — which is now often looking for entertainment in shorter bursts and consumable in moments or minutes instead of portions of hours or longer. TechCrunch
ClickFix attacks are tricking Mac and Windows users into hacking themselves
2026-09-14

ClickFix attacks are tricking Mac and Windows users into hacking themselves

TechCrunch | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt If you clicked on an HBO Max ad on Reddit over the past week, you might want to check your computer for malware. These so-called “ClickFix” attacks have quickly become one of the rising cybersecurity threats of 2026, and they’re getting both sneakier and compromising people’s devices with greater frequency. Until recently, ClickFix attacks were a rarity, capitalizing on people searching the web for quick tech fixes. They have since evolved into a massive international effort to hack into people’s computers. The attacks involve fake websites, or legitimate websites that have been hacked, which display a message that appears to look like a CAPTCHA or an anti-bot checkbox. Once clicked, a prompt appears asking the user to perform a “check” to proceed, which gives instructions to copy and paste a string of text into the user’s Windows command prompt or Mac Terminal app. As soon as the user hits return, they unwittingly and instantly install info-stealing malware on their computer, capable of immediately stealing their passwords, access to their logged-in accounts, and crypto wallets. Since the user is working in the computer’s terminal, which lets them interact directly with the operating system using text-based commands, many of these attacks evade antivirus and security defense tools. Security researchers now say that the latest ClickFix campaign they’ve seen involved hackers posting fake ads on Reddit, linking to a page that looks like HBO Max but contains a ClickFix lure that tricks people into hacking themselves. The hackers compromised the official HBO Max’s account on Reddit that was then used to post hundreds of fake but real-looking adverts to the news-sharing site, according to security researchers at Hudson Rock and a thread on Reddit’s cybersecurity subreddit. It’s unclear how many people clicked on these fake ads or how many were ultimately compromised as a result. Warner Brothers Discovery, which owns HBO, did not respond to a request for comment. Reddit told TechCrunch it “recently learned that an HBO Max account authorized to run advertisements on Reddit was compromised and used to run ads containing malicious links,” and that the company locked the account and removed the ads. When asked, Reddit did not say how many users were targeted or clicked the malicious ads. While it’s typical for developers to run one-line snippets of code in their computer’s terminal, it’s less common for regular users to use the Command Prompt or PowerShell in Windows, or the Terminal in macOS. Companies that run fleets of Windows computers can block access to these features across the entire domain to prevent them from being exploited, per security researcher Kevin Beaumont. As noted by Ars Technica, a tool for Mac users called BlockBlock can also defend against attacks that try to trick Apple users into hacking themselves. Volkswagen’s crazy-efficient EV borrows an idea from Slate
2026-09-14

Volkswagen’s crazy-efficient EV borrows an idea from Slate

TechCrunch | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt Volkswagen took the covers off a new prototype electric vehicle Monday, one that’s capable of hitting the equivalent of 323 miles per gallon under ideal conditions, making it one of the most efficient cars in the world. It’s not unusual for automakers to float pie-in-the-sky concepts with outlandish stats, but Volkswagen took it a step or two further by building the EV and driving it from its home base in Wolfsburg, Germany, on a 794-mile (1,278-kilometer) trip to Vienna. The team charged the Mission Efficiency prototype once during the journey and arrived with 104 miles (168 kilometers) of range left, resulting in consumption of 7.51 kilowatt-hours per 100 miles, or about 279 MPGe. The teardrop-shaped Mission Efficiency prototype was built using the electric motor and battery from the ID. Polo, two features that bring the car to “near production” status, Volkswagen said in a press release. For those keeping score, the prototype is almost twice as efficient as the most efficient production car in the world, the Lucid Air Pure. By some back-of-the-envelope math, Volkswagen’s science experiment can get more than 400 miles of range out of a 54.9-kilowatt-hour battery, though that’s not an official EPA rating. Lucid gets 420 miles from an 84-kilowatt-hour battery per EPA ratings, but it’s a much larger and more practical vehicle. Volkswagen had to make some compromises in the name of efficiency. The most noticeable is the roofline, which tapers quickly after peaking above the driver’s head. The shape, known as a Kammback, is among the most aerodynamic shapes a vehicle can take. Volkswagen was able to bring the coefficient of drag down to 0.158, while also keeping the frontal area low at just 2.08 square meters. As a result, the Mission Efficiency is technically a 2+2, meaning the rear seats aren’t suitable for most adults. Volkswagen said the backseat is designed for people shorter than 5-foot 3 inches, or about 1.6 meters. “The prototype was designed so that it could theoretically be used by a young family of four,” Volkswagen said, though anyone who has contemplated sitting in a 2+2 knows that even kids might scoff at the notion. Another compromise sits closer to the road. Because wheels and tires can contribute about a quarter to a third of aerodynamic drag, the designers tightened the space between the wheel arches and the front tires. It’s great for efficiency, but probably not great for ride quality since wheels need space to move inside the wheel wells so the shocks can absorb bumps. One place Volkswagen didn’t compromise was the mirrors. Sideview mirrors generate a lot of drag just by hanging off the sides, and while cameras would have cut into those losses significantly, they don’t offer the same sense of depth that true mirrors do. To top it all off, Volkswagen put solar cells on the roof and the trunk lid. It’s a nice touch that’s sure to delight the internet, but their impact is limited, adding just 18 miles (30 kilometers) to perfectly flat, sunny routes driven at a constant 42.25 mph (68 kph). Inside, the seats are built using lightweight materials and designs. On the dash, Volkswagen took a page from EV startup Slate Auto. Like Slate’s bare-bones pickup, the Mission Efficiency doesn’t come with an infotainment system or built-in speakers. Instead, it offers a portable Bluetooth speaker and assumes drivers will bring their own devices. While Volkswagen frequently calls the Mission Efficiency a “near production” vehicle, the company didn’t commit to actual production. Though don’t assume it won’t: Over a decade ago, the automaker produced 250 examples of the XL1, another efficiency-minded halo car. And like other halo cars, it didn’t come cheap, with prices starting at €110,000 in 2013. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. TIME
Who Is Umar Kremlev? The Russian Businessman With Ties to Putin Who Partly Funded Trump Jr.’s Wedding Celebrations
2026-09-14

Who Is Umar Kremlev? The Russian Businessman With Ties to Putin Who Partly Funded Trump Jr.’s Wedding Celebrations

TIME | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Russian President Vladimir Putin (L) greets the president of the International Boxing Association Umar Kremlev (R) during a meeting with national boxing team at the Kremlin in Moscow, Russia, on April 22, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A Russian businessman with ties to the country’s President Vladimir Putin footed the bill for celebrations following the wedding of President Donald Trump’s son, Donald Trump Jr., Trump Jr. and his wife, Bettina, have confirmed. Oligarch Umar Kremlev’s contributions to the wedding festivities were first reported by ProPublica, which wrote that the Russian businessman spent hundreds of thousands of dollars to rent out a private island in the Bahamas and a fireworks show. The outlet reported that the guest list included roughly 50 guests, including family members, close friends, and Kremlev, who according to ProPublica was part of a larger group of Russian guests whose presence was a source of confusion for other attendees. Trump Jr. and Bettina Trump shared a statement on social media in response to criticism over his financial involvement in the event, defending what she described as an “extraordinarily generous wedding gift from a friend” and denying any political motive behind Kremlev’s attendance. “Our dear friend Umar very generously hosted two incredible nights of celebrations for us AFTER our wedding,” they wrote in a post on Bettina Trump’s Instagram on Monday in a caption that accompanied a series of photos from the evenings in question showing the couple partying with other guests, performances, and the beach-side venue. “It’s unfortunate that something so personal and happy can be recast as something political or sinister simply because of who someone is or where they come from. Friendship doesn’t require a political motive.” The Trumps’ connection to a figure closely tied to the leadership in Russia—and his apparently lavish financial contribution to the wedding celebrations—has however drawn concerns from Democratic politicians and experts in ethics and intelligence, who have raised questions about what might be expected in return for the "gift" and warned that Russian intelligence services may seek to weaponize such a relationship. Here’s what to know about Kremlev and his ties to Putin and the Trump family. Kremlev, 43, is a native of Tajikistan. He was previously known by his surname at birth, Lutfulloyev, which he changed to Kremlev in 2010 when he was in his late twenties, according to the independent Russian news organization Proekt. Prior to that name change, Kremlev was convicted of extortion in 2004 and battery in 2007. He went on to join a notorious biker gang, the Night Wolves, not long after changing his name, according to Russian news reports. Outlets reported that during his time in the group, which was the first official biker community in the Union of Soviet Socialist Republics (USSR), Kremlev met Alexei Rubezhnoi, who currently leads Putin’s presidential security service. The Washington Post reported that Putin himself is considered an honorary member of the Night Wolves. In 2015, Kremlev, who had boxed since he was a child, founded a company, Patriot, that promoted boxing matches. Two years later, he became the secretary general of the International Boxing Association (IBA) after a shake up led to Rubezhnoi becoming the chairman of its supervisory board. Rubezhnoi then nominated Kremlev to the secretary general position; from there, Kremlev went on to be elected as president of the IBA in 2020. In 2024, Kremlev acquired Rolf, Russia’s largest car dealership group, which had previously been state-owned. “These services,” Triplett says in reference to the Kremlin, “are exceptional at finding pain points and then and then manipulating them, and you can get people into difficult positions.” Kremlev has been connected with Putin for years, as has been documented through public appearances and business dealings. During Kremlev’s time as IBA president, Gazprom, a state-owned energy company and one of the largest companies in Russia, became a financial backer of the boxing federation. That same year, Kremlev stood alongside Putin during the opening of the International Boxing Center in Moscow. Under Putin, many Russian government officials or oligarchs serve in supervisory positions for sports organizations in the country. “Umar is guided by Putin. It was using the sport for soft political power,” an ex-IBA board member told ProPublica of Kremlev’s leadership of the organization. “It’s geopolitics. That it’s boxing is just happenstance.” Following Kremlev’s acquisition of Rolf in 2024, meanwhile, Putin repealed the company’s federal ownership, which he had established through a presidential decree in 2023. He was also honored by Putin with the Order of Friendship, according to the IBA, a state accolade that celebrates those who have helped to improve the Russian Federation in various sectors. And earlier this year, Kemlev accompanied Putin on a visit to China, according to Chinese state media. Kremlev’s press office told ProPublica in a statement that he was not part of the Russian President’s “official delegation” during the trip. Kremlev has been publicly linked to President Trump and Trump Jr. through the IBA in recent months. It’s unclear how else he may be connected with the Trump family. After President Trump returned to the White House last year, Kremlev and the CEO of the boxing organization sent him an open letter congratulating him on his re-election and expressing the IBA’s “sincere gratitude for your strong and principled stance regarding the inadmissibility in the participation of ineligible athletes in women’s boxing competition,” in reference to Trump’s stance against the participation of transgender women in women’s sports. The letter then nodded towards the 2028 Olympic Games, which will be hosted by the U.S. in Los Angeles, saying that the IBA looks ahead “with great optimism to the possibility of working together to make the Olympic movement great again, advance the sport of boxing and to champion the rights of athletes globally.” Last September, Trump Jr. sat on a panel discussion hosted by the IBA in Istanbul, during which he also critiqued the participation of transgender women in women’s sports while onstage with Kremlev. Kremlev’s press office said in its statement to ProPublica that he and Trump Jr. initially met “a couple years ago” and that the two men “have a friendly relationship.” It added that Kremlev “has never discussed political matters with any of his American friends and acquaintances,” Trump Jr. included. A spokesperson for Trump Jr. told the New York Timesthat the two men bonded over boxing and hunting after meeting through a mutual friend, and that they are not in business with one another. The President, who did not attend Trump Jr.’s wedding or the following celebrations, said in a statement provided to the Times on Monday that “I have no idea who Umar is, never heard of him, and he didn’t pay for Don and Bettina’s wedding, which took place at a totally different location, and on a different day.” A spokeswoman for Eric Trump, Trump Jr.’s brother, meanwhile told the Times and ProPublica, “Eric has absolutely no clue who this person is.” “If the allegations are true, then it certainly reflects poor judgment on Donald Trump Jr.’s part,” Dan Hoffman, an intelligence expert with over 30 years of experience working in the Central Intelligence Agency (CIA), U.S. military, Department of State, and Department of Commerce, tells TIME. “It is highly unlikely Umar Kremlev would have any interaction with any member of the Trump family without the Kremlin knowing about it beforehand.” Holden Triplett, who served nearly 15 years with the Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI) and was previously the Director for Counterintelligence at the National Security Council at the White House during Trump’s first Administration, says that any kind of relationship between an individual with ties to the President and someone linked to the Kremlin can pose a risk to the U.S.—though he says he doesn’t find the Trumps’ apparent relationship with Kremlev to be particularly alarming. “Even the most above-board, non-threatening-nefarious relationship could be weaponized by intelligence services,” he tells TIME. “Russians commonly try to use, as a lot of intel agencies do, business relationships as a non-alarming way to establish connection with someone … It is one that the Russians have used with lots of, or attempts at least, with lots of other individuals in politics.” This isn’t the first time Trump Jr. has drawn controversy over interactions related to Russia. During the 2016 presidential campaign, Trump Jr. met with a Russian lawyer who claimed she could provide information that would hurt his father’s Democratic opponent Hillary Clinton’s campaign. That meeting later became a point in the investigation special counsel Robert Mueller conducted into President Trump’s campaign and its ties to Russia, though Trump Jr. was never accused of wrongdoing in the probe.

Mitch McConnell Returns to the Senate, Ending His Months-Long Absence

Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Sen. Mitch McConnell (R-KY) speaks to the press as he arrives for a vote at the U.S. Capitol in Washington, D.C., on Sept. 14, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Sen. Mitch McConnell announced that he’s casting a vote in the Senate on Monday, marking the end of his months-long absence from the upper chamber after he was hospitalized in June following a fall. “Today, I’ll cast my first Senate vote since I took a bad fall back in June,” McConnell said in a post on X on Monday evening. “I’m really looking forward to being back on the Senate floor and seeing my colleagues.” The 84-year-old Kentucky Republican went on to say that his "recovery has been a long and often frustrating process,” adding that the “lingering effects” of his bout with polio during his childhood have also impacted his recovery. “I’m still not quite back to 100%, but I’ve assured [Senate Majority Leader John Thune] that, as I continue with physical therapy on the advice of my doctors, I will do my best to be present for tough votes when our Conference needs me,” McConnell said. McConnell’s office initially shared little to no information when the Senator was hospitalized on June 14. After weeks of uncertainty—and his absence from the Senate—rumors and speculation over the state of his health began swirling online. In July, Democratic Kentucky Gov. Andy Beshear shared a letter in which he urged McConnell to “fully update Kentuckians regarding the current status of [his] health.” Read More: Kentucky Gov. Beshear Keeps Pushing for Info on McConnell’s Health. He Says He’s ‘Heard Absolutely Nothing Back’ A few days later, the Republican lawmaker disclosed that he had been taken to the hospital after he “briefly” lost consciousness following a fall, and that he later had to grapple with a “mild case of pneumonia.” He pushed back, though, on rumors that he was suffering from any severe conditions, saying that his doctors confirmed that he didn’t break any bones or suffer a concussion, and that he didn’t have any tumors or experience a heart attack. At the end of July, McConnell’s office shared another update, stating that the Senator was maintaining his physical therapy, per his doctors’ orders, and that he had been discharged from the hospital but was not, at that time, medically cleared to leave the rehabilitation center he was at and return to the upper chamber. On Aug. 6, his office announced that he had been discharged from the rehabilitation facility that day and would continue his recovery at home. McConnell plans to retire in January, when his term comes to an end. This is not the first time that he has had a health scare; he has had multiple falling incidents in recent years.

Canada Seeks 'Unique Alliance' With E.U. Amid U.S. Trade War. Here's What It Could Look Like
2026-09-14

Canada Seeks 'Unique Alliance' With E.U. Amid U.S. Trade War. Here's What It Could Look Like

TIME | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings European Commission president Ursula von der Leyen and Canada's Prime Minister Mark Carney speak at the G7 summit in Evian-les-Bains, France, on June 16, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney said Canada is seeking a “unique alliance” with the European Union as he aims to strengthen international ties amid a deepening trade war with the U.S. “What we are looking, and will begin discussions for, [is] a unique alliance with the European Union,” Carney told reporters at the Toronto International Film Festival on Sunday. “We share the same values, we have the same priorities, and we have very complementary strengths.” Canada’s European pivot has been months in the making, following growing tensions between Carney’s government and the Trump Administration. Canada’s retaliatory tariffs came into effect last week, matching the economic pressure imposed by the U.S. after negotiations collapsed last month. Carney has encouraged Canadians to take back economic power at the kitchen table, and many have embraced a boycott of U.S. products. Trump has also repeatedly threatened Canada’s sovereignty, speaking openly of his desire to annex the country and make it the “51st state.” The U.S. accounted for 72.5% of Canadian merchandise exports in 2025, according to the country's annual trade report, making it Canada’s largest trading partner. But Carney is keen to build an international support network that isn’t reliant on the U.S. Over the past 18 months since Carney entered office, Canada has signed agreements that cover trade, defense, more with countries such as Indonesia, Japan, and Ecuador. At the E.U.-Canada summit in June 2025, Canada established a new Security and Defense Partnership with the bloc. “Our partnership is grounded in the core values we share: democracy, human rights, the rule of law, and open, rules-based markets," read a joint statement marking the occasion. In January, Carney notably met with China’s President Xi Jinping to forge what he referred to as a “new strategic partnership” to end Canada's economic reliance on the American market. Carney agreed to cut his country’s 100% tariff on Chinese electric cars in return for lower tariffs on Canadian farm products, including canola seeds, a major Canadian export. Continuing with these efforts, Carney on Monday brought together executives and senior leaders from global investment firms to encourage them to invest in the country at the Canada Investment Summit in Toronto. He’s heading for a three-day trip to Europe on Tuesday, where he’ll meet with various European leaders including British Prime Minister Andy Burnham. He’s also set to address the European Parliament in Strasbourg, France, on Thursday, in what could symbolize the mark of a new era between Canada and Europe. Here’s a look at the alliance options on the table between Canada and the bloc, and what that could mean for U.S.-Canada relations. Canada is not looking to join the E.U. as a member, Carney clarified amid questions over what a firmer relationship between his country and the 27-member bloc might look like. “We’re not looking to become a member,” Carney said, emphasizing he’s instead keen to form an alliance. “We’re stronger together, and this is at a time where the world’s more dangerous and divided, and friends need to stick together in these times.” Article 49 of the Treaty on European Union explicitly states that any “European State” can apply for membership, making Canada ineligible. However, a report in the Wall Street Journal, quoting officials from both sides, said Carney is keen for Canada to become an “associate member” of the E.U. When approached for comment, the European Commission directed TIME to remarks made during a Monday press briefing in Brussels. “Canada is a strategically important partner for the European Union, and indeed a close friend,” the European Commission’s deputy chief spokesperson Olof Gill told reporters. “We deeply value this co-operation. You will recall that at our E.U.-Canada summit last year, we set a very high level of ambition for cooperation across the board, this year our ambition is even higher.” When asked directly what discussions had taken place between Canadian and European officials, or what this “unique alliance” could look like, Gill declined to answer. TIME has reached out to the Canadian Prime Minister’s office for comment. Frank Schimmelfennig, a professor of European politics at ETH Zurich, predicts that Canada may wish to pursue deeper cooperation through additional trade agreements, or take inspiration from countries that have close relationships with the bloc without being E.U. members. “Canada is already one of the most integrated non-European countries. It has the densest network of institutional relationships with the E.U.,” he tells TIME, referencing the Comprehensive Economic and Trade Agreement (CETA), a major free-trade deal between the E.U. and Canada, signed in 2016, and Horizon Europe, an initiative on collaborative research launched by the European Union in 2021 and joined by Canada in 2024. “Canada could have more of the same, which means additional sectoral agreements for sectors that haven't been covered yet,” he says. “That would basically be a lateral extension of the current relationship. Anything more, would mean access to the internal market which would require an arrangement like the EEA.” The European Economic Area (EEA) includes countries such as Iceland and Norway, which are not members of the E.U., and provides for the free movement of goods, services, and people within the E.U.’s member states, while enabling cooperation across multiple industries. “The EEA countries have access to the E.U. internal market in return for adopting the relevant E.U. regulations and directives, so that would be a much denser legal integration than what Canada has now,” says Schimmelfennig. At the World Economic Forum in Davos in January, Carney declared a "rupture" in the international rules-based order. He said "great powers" were using tariffs and financial systems as instruments of coercion. He did not directly name Trump or the U.S. The speech came at a moment of acute transatlantic tension over Trump’s push to annex the semi-autonomous Danish territory of Greenland and his short-lived threats to tariff European allies until Denmark relented. Patrick Leblond, an associate professor at the University of Ottawa, says Carney’s movements now are in response to the world “seeing a United States that is becoming more protectionist, nationalist, and willing to use its leverage points to gain concessions, including on very close allies like Canada and Europeans.” “For Canada, [the consensus is] we need to do more with partners who share our values based on liberal democracy, open markets, rule of law, international agreements, and the first port of call for Carney is the European Union,” Leblond tells TIME. These trade efforts, he continues, may allow Carney to “build momentum, and hopefully bring other countries” into a broader coalition of what the Canadian leader has often called “middle powers." In July, Carney, in a joint op-ed with Finland’s President Alexander Stubb, urged the “middle powers of the world” to “unite.” Championing middle power countries that possess significant economic power and diplomatic influence, but lack market or military power, the leaders implored: “In a rapidly changing world, we have the grit and the sisu to chart the path ahead—and we know that so have many others whom we invite to join us on this quest.” Leblod explains that “ultimately trust has been eroded” with the U.S. and Canada must re-think its place in the wider global economy in the long-term. “The first time we thought Trump was a blimp, but he's not, and we're looking at what's happening in terms of democracy and political polarization, and a lot of Canadians think we can't rely on the U.S.,” he says. According to a poll by Nanos Research, conducted between Aug. 30 and Sept. 2, 74% of Canadians disagreed that “the U.S. is a trustworthy ally of Canada.” An earlier poll by Angus Reid Institute, conducted between July 10-14, found that 42% of respondents said they think the Canadian government should approach the U.S. as an enemy/potential threat. The E.U. has also clashed with Trump over tariffs, his Greenland annexation threats, and the wider split between the U.S. President and several European leaders. German Chancellor Friedrich Merz in February warned of “a deep rift” between Europe and the United States, arguing that the latter “will not be powerful enough to go it alone.” But relations have since soured further amid the fallout of the Iran war, particularly between Merz and Trump. Meanwhile, Canada has also embraced Europe in the cultural space. It was announced earlier this year that Canada will ​join the Eurovision Song Contest in 2027, becoming the first new ⁠participant since Australia in 2015. Carney’s highly-anticipated speech at the European Parliament in Strasbourg this week stems from an invitation extended by European Parliament president Roberta Metsola. Leblond predicts Carney will use the speech “to reiterate a lot of the things he mentioned at Davos,” but with a strong, intentional focus on the E.U. “I would expect that he will use that address to call on the E.U. to work with Canada to push this idea of co-operation beyond the North Atlantic and include partners and allies in the Indo-Pacific,” he says. The Canadian leader is also expected to be a guest at the State of the European Union debate, where European Commission president Ursula von der Leyen is due to deliver a speech outlining the Commission's work and plans for the future. Von der Leyen called E.U.–Canada ties “one of the closest partnerships in the world” when she celebrated 50 years of E.U. diplomatic presence in Canada in May. She declared the two sides were “closer than ever” as they confronted wars, an energy crisis and “new fractures in old alliances.” Schimmelfennig says Canada inching closer to the E.U. is a strong display of “how both partners are affected by the deteriorating transatlantic relationship” with the U.S. “They feel that they need to move together because the world is getting more dangerous, and more difficult for both [parties],” he says.

In Gen Z Romance Shows Like Crew Girl, Sports Are the New Sex
2026-09-14

In Gen Z Romance Shows Like Crew Girl, Sports Are the New Sex

TIME | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings From left: Sam Braun, Miku Martineau, and Kyle Clark in Crew Girl Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In the Netflix teen drama Crew Girl, 16-year-old solo rowing champ Teagan Tao has her life uprooted when her hotel-magnate dad gets a tip that he’s about to be arrested for financial crimes and disappears. Suddenly, Teagan (Miku Martineau) and her flighty mom, Ella (Jessica Paré), are broke and moving across the country to the crumbling New England house where Ella grew up. The town is all clam shacks and marinas—and the prestigious Easton Prep, where Teagan matriculates thanks to an education trust that was somehow spared from the seizure of the Taos’ assets. The only problem, besides the rampant snobbery, is that Eaton has no girls’ crew team. Forced to coxswain for the boys, Teagan starts falling for two of her teammates: kind, working-class townie Cam Dillinger (Kyle Clark) and bratty airline scion Josh Regis (Sam Braun), who’s locked in a premature succession battle with his cousin Ryder (Connor Paton). Meanwhile, a gay rower, Baloo (Jude Wilson), is secretly hooking up with a guy on a rival team. All of which is to say that Crew Girl, whose first season is now the no. 1 show on Netflix, plays like a greatest-hits compilation of teen romance tropes. First among the classics is a love triangle (see: Twilight, Riverdale, The Vampire Diaries, My So-Called Life). As is often the case, this one is enhanced by the quaintness of its small-town setting (Dawson’s Creek, One Tree Hill) and complicated by both wealth disparities (Gossip Girl, Pretty in Pink) and one character’s fish-out-of-water, new-kid-in-school status (The O.C., Beverly Hills, 90210). There’s also a youthful mom (you might remember Paré as Mad Men’s Megan) doing a painfully slow will-they-won’t-they dance with an old friend (Thomas Cadrot), à la Gilmore Girls’ Lorelai and Luke. From left: Jarrett Carlington, Jude Wilson, Connor Paton, Kyle Clark, and Miku Martineau in Crew Girl Netflix But what’s remarkable about Crew Girl—which is, despite an effervescent lead and a few fun storylines built around eccentric minor crew-boy characters, otherwise decidedly mid—is how comprehensively it showcases the conventions unique to the genre’s newest generation. TV has, in the last few years, capitalized on publishing’s pandemic-era romance boom, churning out love stories tailored to the young, female audience driving authors like Emily Henry and Ali Hazelwood up best-seller lists. Often, that means adapting popular YA or New Adult novels; Prime Video’s success with Jenny Han’s coming-of-age, beach-town love triangle The Summer I Turned Pretty paved the way for Netflix’s take on Ali Novak’s My Life with the Walter Boys as well as XO, Kitty, Han’s spinoff of her book-to-film franchise To All the Boys I’ve Loved Before. Queer romance, mainly of the gay male variety, from Netflix’s tender and chaste Heartstopper to the spicy Canadian phenomenon Heated Rivalry, which streams on HBO Max in the U.S., has also jumped from page to screen. (Crew Girl isn’t an adaptation, but a novelization released in tandem with the show suggests it’s meant to feel like one.) This year has seen a particular glut of new titles, from Netflix’s figure-skating drama Finding Her Edge to Prime’s Off Campus and Every Year After. The latter platform even scored a hit in August with an exceptional original teen romance, Sterling Point, proving the niche isn’t entirely dependent on preexisting IP. These shows have all the love triangles and class struggles and outsider angst that have defined youthful romance tales since Jane Austen was writing them, capturing eternal adolescent fantasies (being desirable enough to have two people fighting over you) and fears (not measuring up to the rich kids) alike. Pretty appears to have sparked a mini-trend of siblings competing for the same crush, a trope that also pops up in Every Year After, Walter Boys, and Finding Her Edge. The fraught post-DEI-backlash politics of representation, especially for the most racially and ethnically diverse generation on record, are evident in the shows’ leads. Like Crew Girl’s Martineau, many are multiracial but play characters with vaguely defined backgrounds who live with either a single white parent or a white surrogate family. (New York magazine just spotlighted the "Wasian generation," a long list of up-and-coming white and Asian stars, citing Heated Rivalry’s Hudson Williams, Pretty’s Lola Tung, and XO, Kitty’s Anna Cathcart among other white and Asian stars.) Liberal and minority viewers get to see mixed-race families and romances, and a TV landscape that isn’t all white. The anti-”woke” crowd is spared identity politics, non-English dialogue, and often all other forms of cultural specificity. Savvy streamers avoid backlash from both sides. Connor Storrie, left, and Hudson Williams in Heated Rivalry HBO Max What’s most striking about these series, though, are the novel ways in which they embrace physicality. With the steamy exceptions of Heated Rivalry and Off Campus (both geared toward a slightly older age range), the sexual content is pretty PG-13. Objectification is kept to a minimum, at least by characters we’re supposed to like. Yet bodies are conspicuously activated, in what feel like pointed responses to—and, for young viewers, escape hatches from—digital disembodiment. Summer romances, and specifically those that bring together wealthy vacationers and the locals who work for them, aren’t new to fiction. It’s the prominence of nature in the recent crop of teen romances (and teen hits in other genres, like Netflix’s Outer Banks) that makes an impression. As it toggles between protagonist Percy’s (Sadie Soverall) life in her late 20s and a sequence of idyllic teenage lake-house summers glimpsed in flashbacks, Every Year After uses its setting to visualize two brothers’ rivalry for her affection. In one recurring image, Percy sits at the edge of her house’s dock, looking on as sensitive Sam (Matt Cornett) and arrogant Charlie (Michael Bradway) shove each other off a slippery wooden raft. Sterling Point brings a high-strung New York City girl (Ella Rubin) to an island in Canada’s Muskoka region, a paradise of lush, lakefront forests. Her rich-vs.-poor-boy love triangle plays out over lazy days spent boating, swimming, and sunbathing on rocky beaches. Cellphone service is conveniently spotty. An abundance of sports romances make such physicality even more explicit. In Off Campus and Heated Rivalry, the boys who serve as eye candy for any viewer attracted to men are always slamming into each other on the ice and changing together in the locker room. In Finding Her Edge, a show about young Olympics hopefuls at a residential training center, an ice-dancing duo is bound to become a love affair (as long as the guy is into girls). Even the neurotic hero of Heartstopper (Joe Locke) finds first love on the rugby field. Crew Girl casts many thirsty gazes on the musclebound upper bodies of Teagan’s teammates. These stories aren’t just about the sweaty sensuality of athletes in motion, though. They’re about ambition and passion and the competitive spirit as romantic accelerants—about how the shared commitment to spend hours every day striving for excellence while one’s peers are screwing around online can bond people. Belmont Cameli and Ella Bright in Off Campus Liane Hentscher—Prime Why use athletics as a fig leaf for sex in the uncensored age of streaming, years after shows like Euphoria and Skins obliterated the notion that TV for teens should be wholesome? Maybe because modesty is in. Gen Z’s squeamishness about sex on screen has fueled successive waves of hand-wringing among their elders. The so-called sex recession plaguing this cohort in real life has been duly noted, with suggested explanations ranging from extreme onlineness in general to poor socialization during the COVID years of Zoom school and virtual prom in particular. The thing is, biologically speaking, teens are the same jumble of hormones in 2026 that they were during the Summer of Love. “Many of them are very horny,” Carter Sherman, the author of The Second Coming: Sex and the Next Generation's Fight Over Its Future, assured WIRED last year. “They would like to be having sex, and in fact they feel a lot of shame over the fact that they haven't had sex yet or that they’re not having sex enough.” As transitional objects to ease anxieties around physical intimacy, swimsuits and erg machines make a lot of sense. The most salient takeaway from Crew Girl and its ilk might be buried in a less eye-catching finding of 2025’s Teens and Screens, the annual UCLA report on young people’s attitudes toward entertainment media that reliably stokes the “Gen Z hates sex scenes” panic. Relatability is the new watchword. Whereas millennials wallowed in wealth porn, took vicarious flight with superheroes, and sucked the life out of vampire content, today’s adolescents ranked “people with lives like mine” as the No. 1 element they’re looking for in TV shows and movies. Although they’re aspirational enough to feel escapist, with their summer houses and prep-school popularity contests, this season’s teen romances reflect that shift. For a generation raised in the sensory deprivation tank of the internet, whose fears of infinite digital liminality just gave us the horror movies Backrooms and It Ends, the fantasy is humbler than superpowers. It’s being grounded in physical space, a human body capable of sharing tactile experiences with other embodied people. Sex is one way to make those connections. Crew is another.

How The Early Spring Ends—And Sets the Stage for More Chinese Dramas on Netflix
2026-09-14

How The Early Spring Ends—And Sets the Stage for More Chinese Dramas on Netflix

TIME | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Shang Zhitao (Sun Qian) and Luan Nian (Jing Boran) in The Early Spring Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A happy ending for Shang Zhitao (Sun Qian) and Luan Nian (Jing Boran) on Netflix’s Chinese workplace romance The Early Spring was incredibly hard-won. At the start of the 24-episode series based on a web novel by Gu Niang Bie Ku, recent college graduate Zhitao is hired by Beijing advertising agency Lime—against bigwig Nian’s wishes. The initial power imbalance between ordinary office worker Zhitao and her boss Nian is significant, and there is a company policy against romantic entanglements between co-workers. Still, they quickly fall into bed together. Over the next six years, the two are on-again, off-again as a prideful, emotionally repressed Nian struggles to say nice things to Zhitao and Zhitao struggles to balance her love for Nian with her commitment to her career. For Zhitao, Beijing isn’t just a place—it is a symbol of her career ambitions, and the kind of life she aspires to. While The Early Spring’s central focus is romance, the C-drama also delves into other aspects of Zhitao’s character and journey. A major throughline of the show is Zhitao’s efforts to make Beijing a home, and to succeed not just at a company or in a bustling metropolis, but in one of the most prestigious companies in one of China’s most global cities. So, when Zhitao chooses to leave Beijing in Episode 21, “A Grand Retreat,” she isn’t just retreating from Nian, she is retreating from the lifestyle that Beijing demands for success. It’s not just one event that informs Zhitao’s decision to leave Beijing. When she tells Nian that she wants to get married and he tells her he doesn’t really think about it, Zhitao begins to reconsider their relationship. Then, one of Zhitao’s best friends dies by suicide. Finally, she gets passed over for a promotion in favor of a less experienced, nepo-baby co-worker. When she seeks comfort from Nian, he offers none. It’s the final straw for Zhitao, who begins to feel like all the time and energy she has committed to their relationship might not have been worth it. Heading into the final installments of the drama, Zhitao has finally called it quits on her toxic dynamic with Nian, moving home to Harbin and starting her own advertising agency in the snowy, northeastern city where she grew up. A few years pass before a bereft Nian attempts to reconcile. Zhitao’s company has partnered with Lime for a local project, and Nian uses it as an excuse to see Zhitao again. Zhitao is understandably reluctant to let Nian back into her life, but Nian uses the excuse of their dog, a Samoyed named Luke, to spend time with his ex-girlfriend. Over the following months, Nian elbows his way into Zhitao’s life and attempts to show her that he has changed. He apologizes for his past behavior, and mostly listens to her when she asks for space. Eventually, Zhitao’s love for Nian wins out and she lets him back into her heart and her life. Nian makes it clear to Zhitao’s parents that he intends to marry their daughter, and then proposes to Zhitao by decorating an art gallery with photos from their time together. He calls it Flora’s Museum of Time and Eternity, using the English name Zhitao used when she worked at Lime. Lian gifts Zhitao a necklace with the “L” key from the typewriter Zhitao knocked over in his office during their first in-person meeting. He invites Zhitao’s closest friends and family, and asks the woman he has loved for almost a decade to marry him. She says yes, finally believing that the two can have a relationship as equals. In the final scenes of the show, we see Zhitao and Nian splitting their time between Harbin and Beijing, happy together. The lovers at the center of The Early Spring Courtesy of Netflix The Early Spring has done incredibly well on Youku and Netflix, where the C-drama has steadily been in the Global Top Ten list. East Asian dramas don’t tend to have second seasons, but The Early Spring could easily be extended with the adaptation of the second book in the web novel series. Titled Miss Talkative, the sequel novel follows the unlikely romance between Zhitao’s work bestie Lumi and her stoic, more traditional co-worker Will. A developing relationship between the two is hinted at in The Early Spring TV series, but the plot line is left unresolved. A scene from the new show's finale Courtesy of Netflix Though Netflix has become known as a home for Korean dramas, with the U.S. streamer investing billions of dollars into the East Asian country’s TV and film industries, the same has not been true for Chinese dramas. China is not interested in giving any U.S. company too much power in its economy, which has kept the streamer from setting up shop in the country. In 2016, Netflix released a letter to shareholders alongside a quarterly earnings report stating: “The regulatory environment for foreign digital content services in China has become challenging. We now plan to license content to existing online service providers in China rather than operate our own service in China in the near term.” That same year, the Chinese government implemented a de facto ban on South Korean entertainment in China following the announcement that the U.S. would be installing an anti-missile system in South Korea. Netflix is still not legally available in China. In the decade since, Netflix’s catalogue and business model have become increasingly international. As Netflix focuses on expansion into international markets, C-dramas have become an increasingly valuable asset. While C-dramas have a growing audience in the U.S., they are even more popular in countries like Pakistan, Bangladesh, Taiwan, Hong Kong, Thailand, Indonesia, Malaysia, Singapore, and Vietnam. “Audiences in Asia have long loved this category, but we're making them accessible to an even bigger global viewership,” Maya Huang, Head of Chinese-Language Content at Netflix, tells TIME. Netflix has been growing their Mainland China offerings since 2024. Recent hits have included historical fantasy romance Pursuit of Jade and costume drama Ashes to Crown, adding to the organic popularity of earlier series like The Untamed, a historical fantasy romance based on a BL web novel that blew up in popularity during the pandemic. As Netflix looks towards the future, C-dramas are part of their plan, with “a team dedicated to finding the most authentic and best-in-class Chinese language stories” including ones—like The Early Spring—that are simulcast with a Chinese streamer. “With Netflix's reach, dubs and subtitles, and our ability to release titles simultaneously worldwide, we're seeing audiences discover these stories in the U.S., South America, Europe and the Middle East,” says Huang. “We want to give these fans more of what they love—so there's plenty more to come from our Chinese language slate.”
