Why You Should Never Wear Shoes in Your House
2026-09-14

Why You Should Never Wear Shoes in Your House

TIME | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Every time you welcome a guest into your home, you may be letting in thousands of other ones as well—if you allow your visitors to wear their shoes when they enter. The world outside your door is a messy place—full of bacteria, viruses, and environmental toxins—and the soles of your shoes can pick up a lot. According to one 2023 CBS News/YouGov poll, 63% of Americans regularly remove their shoes when they get home, and 24% ask their guests to do the same. This year, the Indiana Department of Health launched a “Shoes Free Protects You and Me” ad campaign about the benefits of a no-shoes policy at home, targeting tracked-in lead in particular. How serious is the risk that shoes pose, and should your house be a no-shoes zone? The published research on shoe contamination is not extensive, but some stands out. In one 2008 study, Charles Gerba, professor of virology at the University of Arizona, studied 26 pairs of shoes that had been worn for three months in the outside world and came up with some alarming results. The shoes carried between 3,600 and 8 million colony-forming units of bacteria, both on the inside and outside of the shoes. E. coli, or fecal bacteria, was the most common organism being tracked around. “[About] 80% of the bottoms of the shoes had fecal bacteria on them,” Gerba wrote in an email to TIME, “largely picking them up from the floors of public restrooms, which always have fecal bacteria on them. They may also pick them up outside, in places that dogs and birds are.” According to Gerba’s numbers, shoes are significantly more germ-ridden than public toilet seats, which have an average of 3,200 colony forming bacterial units. E. coli is not the only infectious agent that shoes carry around. According to University of Houston researchers, 40% of shoes tested carry C. difficile, an intestinal bacterium that causes diarrhea. Health care settings are particularly germ-filled. One 2016 meta-analysis in the Journal of Applied Microbiology surveyed 13 studies, tracing where shoes have traveled and what they picked up there. A small study in that group found that after physicians did rounds in hospitals, 65% of their shoes tested positive for either antibiotic-resistant Staphylococcus aureus (MRSA) or Enterococcus faecalis, another gastrointestinal bacterium. Another found that 48% of shoes worn in operating rooms tested positive for at least one pathogenic bacterium, most commonly Staphylococcus. Rural areas, where livestock, birds, and other animals are common, are ready sources of Salmonella and other bacteria. One 2009 study conducted in rural Alaska had people put on sterilized boots, then walk along a randomly selected outdoor path, and finally step onto a piece of sterilized linoleum and remove the boots. From just that walk, 70% of the boots were positive for multiple gut bacteria. People then wore the contaminated boots on short walks across a sterile floor. On half of those walks, bacteria were transferred to the flooring. Urban settings present their own perils, particularly as a result of dogs being walked on sidewalks and in parks. Even in cities where owners routinely pick up after their dogs, traces of fecal bacteria—and pools of urine—regularly collect on heavily foot-trafficked routes. In one 2023 study published in Design, Architecture, and Ergonomics, investigators from Northeastern University in New York City sampled sidewalk rain puddles, shoe bottoms, and flooring inside one of the school’s buildings on its urban campus. On average, they found 30,000 gut bacteria per 100 milliliters of water in puddles on the neighborhood sidewalks. Every shoe sole they sampled from student and teacher volunteers carried varying levels of the same bacteria, and the interior of the building was also contaminated—especially in carpeted areas. “The surface of your flooring makes an enormous difference,” says Alessandra Leri, professor of chemistry and biology at the school and the lead author of the study. “If you have carpets, they function as a reservoir of bacteria.” But no matter the interior floor surface, she says, “shoe soles are a serious vector of transmission of fecal bacteria from the outdoor environment to the indoor environment.” PFAS, the “forever chemicals” so named for how long they linger in the environment and our bodies, are regularly picked up by shoes and used in manufacturing the soles. Lead dust, which can often be stirred up and released into the air during remodeling of older homes and other buildings, can cling to shoes as well. Pesticides, used both on farms and in home gardens, are similarly free-riders on our shoes. In most homes in which people do not remove their shoes upon entering, the area nearest the front door is generally the most contaminated. “We found that the first four steps at the entrance to the household are the germiest floor area in the home,” says Gerba. “We also did tracer studies putting dye and tracer viruses on the bottom of shoes and could see how they left a trail on the floor.” Wherever contaminants spread in the home, they present the greatest danger to the household members who have the most contact with the floor—typically babies and small children who crawl and play on surfaces. Leri follows a no-shoes policy in her home and leaves her shoes by the door when she enters. She does not ask guests to remove their shoes, especially when she hosts parties. “If you are having a cocktail party, you are sensitive to guests feeling like shoes are a part of their ensemble,” she says. But she does roll up the rugs before company arrives, making it easier to clean the floors when the guests are gone. More people—especially younger people—may be taking a harder line, enforcing a universal ban on shoes for anyone in the home. While only 7% of people over 65 and 13% of people ages  45 to 64 require guests to remove shoes before entering, according to the CBS/YouGov poll, 44% of people in the 18 to 29 demographic do. “I find that many people have already adopted that custom,” says Leri. “It is a more hygienic way of living.”

How Long Can the U.S. Escape an Atlantic Hurricane?
2026-09-14

How Long Can the U.S. Escape an Atlantic Hurricane?

The Atlantic | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
This is an edition of The Atlantic Daily, a newsletter that guides you through the biggest stories of the day, helps you discover new ideas, and recommends the best in culture. Sign up for it here. Every president likes to take credit for good news on their watch, but most of them have at least a little shame about it. Not Donald Trump. September 10 is typically the peak of Atlantic Ocean hurricane season. The National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration expected a relatively restrained year, but as the meteorologist and hurricane specialist Michael Lowry writes, the season “has never gone beyond September 11th in the reliable records (since at least 1950) without a hurricane” in the Atlantic Ocean—until this year. There aren’t even any strong prospects forming. Amid spreading violence in the Middle East, warnings of an AI apocalypse, and high fuel prices, it’s a rare piece of good news, and Trump wants credit. Over the weekend, the White House flagged the news, commenting, “Thank you, President Trump!” This is absurd, though maybe no one should be surprised that a man who believed that marking a map with Sharpie can change a hurricane’s path would take credit for a low-key storm season. (He also reportedly once suggested attacking storms with nuclear weapons, so perhaps, as the journalist Catherine Rampell jokes, this is deterrence in action.) Better explanations than presidential benevolence exist. As my colleague Joshua Partlow has reported, this is an unusual year for the El Niño cycle. In the Pacific Ocean, it has produced very warm water, as well as a number of hurricanes. Hawaii, which is seldom hit, has been struck by two hurricanes so far this year. At least four deaths have been attributed to the storms, and power remains out on some parts of the island of Kauai. But scientists say that El Niño also accounts for the absence of major storms, so far, in the Atlantic. “It’s really staggering, just how inhospitable the Atlantic is to anything that could form a tropical cyclone right now,” Kristen Corbosiero, a professor of atmospheric science at the University at Albany, told PBS. The lull is especially notable because last year’s hurricane season was also astonishingly mild in the United States. Although parts of the Caribbean endured hurricanes, not a single one hit the U.S. mainland. This is great news for the Americans who are otherwise often in hurricanes’ paths, and who have been slammed by a series of storms in the past decade—including 2024’s Hurricane Helene, the deadliest to hit the mainland United States since Hurricane Katrina, in 2005. It is also great news for Trump, who must be one of the luckiest people in history. Hurricanes can help or, more often, badly hurt a president. George W. Bush’s reputation never recovered from Katrina, whereas Barack Obama’s steady hand during Hurricane Sandy seemed to bolster his reelection campaign. Trump’s handling of storms during his first term was poor; he demonstrated little human warmth and, in Puerto Rico after Hurricane Maria, jarring coldness. Trump’s personal behavior aside, his administration has been especially fortunate because under his leadership, FEMA appears to be in no shape to handle a major disaster. In the first months of his second presidency, Trump and then–Secretary of Homeland Security Kristi Noem announced plans to dismantle FEMA entirely, devolving responsibility for relief to states, an idea panned by most experts. In the meantime, he seemed determined to slowly strangle it, appointing interim leaders with no disaster-relief experience. First came Cameron Hamilton, who was fired after telling Congress that he did not support liquidating FEMA. Next was David Richardson, who, shortly after taking his post in May 2025, acknowledged that he had no completed plan for the impending hurricane season and would reuse the previous year’s. (FEMA employees reported that Richardson said he didn’t even realize such a season existed; a FEMA spokesperson later said he was joking.) Because no storms have since struck the mainland, these failures ended up having much less human and economic cost. FEMA appears to be on a somewhat stronger footing now. Trump has backed off plans to eliminate FEMA, Noem and Richardson are gone, and Hamilton is back as permanent administrator—and has won some tentative praise for working to learn more about disaster relief. He inherits serious challenges, though. A federal judge ruled on Friday that an administration order to lay off half of FEMA’s workforce was illegal. “Frankly, the FEMA staffing plan number appears as if pulled from thin air,” the judge wrote of the government’s proposal, which included cutting thousands of on-call employees who rush in with aid after a disaster. A report released last month by the Government Accountability Office was even more scathing. The GAO found that FEMA’s personnel decisions had been made “without assessing whether it had the necessary staffing capacity to meet its mission,” including what it is required to do under law, and that “FEMA is not positioned to determine whether it has the right number of people with the right skills in the right positions at the right time to meet its mission.” Furthermore, the report said, “the agency has no overall strategic direction.” Alas, the calm before the storms won’t last indefinitely. Experts who spoke with The New York Times said that it would be premature to assume that no major Atlantic hurricanes will form this year. If the president’s political luck continues, then perhaps the East Coast won’t see a really bad storm until after the midterm elections, or maybe even next year. Given his administration’s approach to hurricane preparedness and the travails of FEMA, though, ordinary Americans along the Atlantic coast and the Gulf Coast are likely to see their weather luck run out. Here are four new stories from The Atlantic: The Biggest Obstacle to Universal Child Care: Babies Last year seemed like a pretty big one for breakthroughs in child care. In New York City, Zohran Mamdani’s promise of publicly funded care for young kids helped him win the keys to Gracie Mansion. Officials in Connecticut created an endowment to make care more affordable in that state. And New Mexico became the first state to commit to free child care for all of its residents. But as policy makers have begun hammering out the details of these ambitions, they’ve hit against a stubborn and formidable obstacle: babies. Take a closer look. Before the photographer Edward Steichen left painting behind forever, he attempted to refocus it on nature, Susan Tallman writes. In a galaxy far, far away. The Star Wars creator George Lucas’s next great epic is an art museum, James Parker writes. Rafaela Jinich contributed to this newsletter. When you buy a book using a link in this newsletter, we receive a commission. Thank you for supporting The Atlantic.

Trump’s Kennedy Center Shakedown
2026-09-14

Trump’s Kennedy Center Shakedown

The Atlantic | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
The saga of Donald Trump’s repeated attempts to slap his name onto the John F. Kennedy Center for the Performing Arts will not go away. In May, Trump lost in court and was ordered to remove his name from the building. But now—according to a 57-page presentation by Trump’s hand-picked executive board obtained by The Washington Post—the Kennedy Center is warning that it will likely go bankrupt. Unless, that is, Trump gets to put his name back on the wall next to that of his slain predecessor. Trump’s support hinges on getting public credit for his involvement. The financial resolution says Trump has offered to “raise the necessary funds to keep the Center from bankruptcy” while the building undergoes a sweeping renovation. But it says Trump is unlikely to provide “fundamental oversight” of that project or lead a financial rescue without “appropriate recognition” of his role. That’s not an offer of help; it’s a shakedown—or, in more direct terms, political blackmail. Two lawyers who have been fighting the Trump administration, Norm Eisen and Nathaniel Zelinsky, said in a statement: “Trump is taking the Center hostage and threatening to in effect kill it unless he gets his way.” The president is counting on the board’s civic spirit and affection for the institution—neither of which he evidently possesses—to strong-arm it into giving him what the law and courts would not allow. Even more galling than Trump’s attempt to pressure the trustees is that his offer is a response to a crisis mostly of his own creation. It’s true that the Kennedy Center, opened more than a half century ago, needs investment and repairs. But the plummeting ticket sales are a result of Trump’s hostile takeover. Performers and patrons alike did not want to legitimize the obscenity of placing Trump’s name on an institution meant to honor Kennedy, and they voted with their feet and their dollars. This very public repudiation of Trump by a slice of the American cultural elite appears to have been an embarrassment to the president; Trump then claimed that he needed to close the center for two years (ostensibly for a complete reconstruction) rather than suffer further humiliation. (Trump, apparently, could not submit to the public indignity of having his name removed from the building in June. He—or someone seeking to please him—ordered workers to cover the building’s wall with tarps at 3 o’clock in the morning so that no one would see the removal. The tarps remain in place.) Read: Dead-mall vibes at the Kennedy Center The only way to avert letting the center go bankrupt, according to the executive board, is to let Trump raise money—and heaven knows what kind of ethical issues that effort would entail—and then to add his name to thank him. The presentation to the board suggests 10 possible statements to place on the building, including “Renovation Restoration and Endowment overseen by President Donald J. Trump and the Trump Kennedy Center Fund.” Putting Trump’s name in front of Kennedy’s is an especially offensive touch, and would enshrine the idea that the center would no longer be named solely for Kennedy. The American people do not support Trump’s mania for renaming things, of which the Kennedy Center is just one example. (The White House itself has become a public exhibit of Trump’s garish taste, including gold script identifying The Oval Office, as if it’s a function room at one of his resorts that needs to be labeled in case a tipsy wedding guest gets lost.) All of this architectural bombast and glitz are expressions both of ego and of insecurity: Trump wants to leave his mark—and his name—on a city that has never accepted him and that will almost certainly try to scrub away his influence when he is gone. The president admitted as much recently, when the writer Ben Terris asked him whether he is building monuments to himself because he is worried that no one else will build them when he’s gone. “That’s true,” Trump said. “Nobody will do it once I’m gone. When I leave here, nobody will.” The president’s admission suggests a way to deal with his attempted shakedown of the Kennedy Center board: take his dare. Read: The Kennedy Center has a problem that courts can’t fix The center’s top officials, the people who were supposed to be entrusted with the future of this cultural icon, have admitted their failure. They acknowledge that Trump will not support the institution unless his name is on it. Well, so be it. The trustees should let the center close, declare bankruptcy, and come to a sad end—all on Trump’s watch. Allow the Kennedy Center to fail in full view of the American public, and let them decide whether such things matter. Otherwise, Trump will plow ahead and do something no one wants, because no one bothered to stop him. The Kennedy Center can always be rebuilt and reopened. Indeed, the restoration of the center could be one of the items on the next president’s to-do list. Scrap the czarist-style ballroom. Rebuild the East Wing of the White House. Halt the fascist-flavored victory arch that will dishonor nearby Arlington Cemetery. Fix the Reflecting Pool. Chisel Trump’s name off the U.S. Institute of Peace. (If the president wants to land at Donald J. Trump International Airport in Palm Beach when he leaves office, that’s between him and the people of Florida.) Painful as it would be to see the Kennedy Center go bankrupt, it would be more painful to cave to Trump’s deeply cynical refusal to save a national treasure unless he gets his name on it. In a famous 1911 poem that gave the world another name for blackmail—danegeld, a reference to the protection money that medieval English villages paid, often in vain, to Danish raiders—Rudyard Kipling warned powerful countries against caving to extortionary threats. The Kennedy Center board, and the American people, should heed Kipling’s advice: For the end of that game is oppression and shame,

A Bigoted Outburst Against Texas Sports Fans
2026-09-14

A Bigoted Outburst Against Texas Sports Fans

The Atlantic | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
On Saturday night, college-football fans in Texas were blessed with a moment of collective ecstasy. Their Longhorns were playing Ohio State, the nation’s No. 1 team, and trailed by 20 points. Then they improbably scored three touchdowns in the fourth quarter. A frenzy shook the stadium as more than 100,000 people, most in team colors, celebrated the stunning comeback. But Bo French, the Republican nominee for a seat on the Texas Railroad Commission, was upset by what he saw in the crowd. “I heard UT graduation this year looked like this,” he wrote yesterday on X, where he posted an image of a cluster of fans at the stadium who appeared to be of South Asian ancestry. “I didn’t believe it. The problem is now obviously far worse than anyone imagined.” Dispelling any doubts about his meaning, he continued: “I weep for the families in Texas who can’t get into UT because they prioritize foreigners over Texans.” He added, “Like body-cams exposed criminals, social media is exposing how many Americans have been displaced by foreigners.” In reality, there is no reason to assume that the students in the photograph are foreigners, and Texas does not prioritize foreigners over Texans. Quite the contrary. Ninety percent of slots for first-year students at UT Austin are reserved for Texas residents. Foreign students even pay higher tuition, which effectively acts as a subsidy for Texas residents at the college. But some people are so psychologically distressed by diversity and difference that their reactions have little to do with the facts on the ground. Some prominent Republicans have objected to the latest––but not the only––bigoted remarks from French. Senator John Cornyn of Texas, who has held office since 2002, posted on X, “Are other Republican elected officials going to condemn the intolerance and racism, or look the other way while it becomes normalized.” He warned, “This is how great political parties self destruct.” But others seemed more incensed by Cornyn’s critique than they were by French’s remarks. A Republican state legislator from North Dakota wrote, “Eat shit you fucking loser.” John Doyle, who has a show for the populist-right outlet Blaze Media, wrote, “For now we can ignore how unqualified you are to speak on behalf of ‘The Republican Party’ as someone who reflexively embraces liberal moralizing about ‘racism’ and ‘intolerance’ in response to a Patriot acknowledging that Texans didn’t ask for their state to become a playground for the third world.” Changes to content moderation on X under Elon Musk’s ownership––not to mention rhetoric and actions within the federal government––have made clear how common bigoted identitarianism is in the GOP coalition. Republicans who believe in the values of Christianity, the creed of the Declaration of Independence, or the moral imperative to judge people as individuals need to confront the prejudice in their ranks. The identitarian right’s rise has lessons for progressives too. First, seeking to raise the salience of race and ethnicity in American politics and culture was a mistake––as ought to have been clear by 2016, when Donald Trump exploited that heightened salience for his own ends. As the political scientist Karen Stenner argued in her 2005 book, causing people who are uncomfortable with difference to perceive it as a threat to social cohesion can activate a latent predisposition toward intolerance. Second, not only was embracing race as a factor in university admissions unpopular with voters, so much so that it lost ballot initiatives even in left-leaning California; colleges’ discrimination against white people and Asians also stoked racial resentment by putting identity groups in a zero-sum competition. And third, once the left began to treat mere departures from left-identitarian shibboleths as racist, it became harder to stigmatize actual racism or xenophobia, as surely as crying wolf at every shih tzu, tabby cat, and hamster would make it harder to warn against actual wolves on the hunt. The Republican and Democratic Parties could do well in future elections to revive a straightforward, morally sound position: Judge others by the content of their character, not by the color of their skin. That ethos needn’t foreclose all disagreements about immigration. And one needn’t pretend not to notice racial and ethnic diversity. But insofar as a diverse crowd cheering for a Longhorn victory implies anything about past or present immigration and its effects, it is this: People of all races and ethnicities have already assimilated into the quasi-religion of Texas football, one of America’s most distinctive cultural phenomena. If, heaven forbid, this great multiethnic nation is ever invaded by a foreign power, there is evidence that the people in that Texas stadium could unite in common cause without regard to race or ancestry. Bo French, who feels distress at seeing his neighbors come together, may lack that ability––a weakness that makes him unfit for office in America.

Gas Prices Could Cost Republicans the Midterms
2026-09-14

Gas Prices Could Cost Republicans the Midterms

The Atlantic | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
One might expect Donald Trump to be concerned about gas prices. Less than two months out from the midterms, gas costs 37 percent more than it did a year ago. Home-heating oil was up 52 percent as of August, and diesel is up a staggering 69 percent. None of those numbers is likely to go down significantly as long as the Iran war drags on. Meanwhile, just last week, a Saudi oil pipeline that carries about 4 percent of the global oil supply shut down after being bombed. (No one has claimed credit for the attack.) But Trump says he isn’t worried. Speaking with reporters last week, he insisted that the United States, not Iran, is in control of the Strait of Hormuz. “Right after the election,” he said, “oil prices are going to be tumbling downward.” Even if that prediction were based on anything resembling fact, the timing would be too late to do Trump much good. Political scientists tend to agree that gas prices influence voter behavior. Although many voters no doubt understand that the president isn’t usually directly responsible for the price of oil, this time, he is. In fact, the oil shock related to Trump’s war of choice with Iran is the only reason the U.S. has any inflation to speak of. Inflation in all non-energy consumer purchases is at just 2 percent over the past year. If Democrats manage to take back the House and Senate in November despite an inhospitable electoral map, the Trump effect on gas prices may well be the reason. Jonathan Chait: Does Trump want Republicans to win the midterms? Voters hate inflation, and gas prices are especially hard to tune out. Juan Felipe Riaño, an economist who has studied inflation’s political consequences, told me that voters understand that price increases translate into lower real wages. The more they drive, the more pronounced the effect. People who live in rural areas and exurbs have the most to lose, because they have the longest commutes. These are the reddest parts of the country, meaning that they are home to millions of voters whom Democrats are trying to persuade to vote blue. According to a Brookings Institution analysis, the average voter in a Republican-held district drives 26 percent more miles than their counterpart in a Democratic district. Other research has found that those with longer drive times are more likely to hold the president accountable for increases in gas prices. These Trump-friendly areas are exactly where Democrats need to make inroads. They can’t take the Senate without winning multiple states that Trump won by more than 10 points in 2024. Meanwhile, although gerrymandering has created more red districts, it has done so by spreading the Republican advantage more thinly across districts. This has created more places where Democrats could win in a wave election. Currently, Democrats lead Republicans on generic congressional polls by about seven points. In a plus-seven election, their best recruits could pick up seats even in districts that went heavily for Trump in 2024. Perhaps the best thing Trump could do to avoid that outcome would be to bring gas prices down before Election Day. His approval rating has been declining almost continuously since he took office a second time, and since August, it has hovered around 38 percent, according to Silver Bulletin. Voters say that their top issue is the cost of living, and only 25 percent approve of how Trump has handled it. That’s a decline of nine points since the war with Iran began. Sarah Longwell: Voters have a message for Democrats As for Republican candidates in competitive races, their best bet for avoiding the gas-price backlash might be to distance themselves from the president. But Trump has made that extremely difficult. Last week, Republicans gathered in Dallas for an unusual midterm convention devoted to celebrating Trump’s greatness. At one point, he asked the crowd to repeat a “pledge to the greatest president in the history of the United States.” And if Trump’s goal is to remind Republican candidates that their voters’ first loyalty is to him, not them, he seems to be succeeding. Only a tiny handful of congressional Republicans have publicly denounced the Iran war. Several vulnerable Republicans have refused to allow daylight between themselves and Trump, even when doing so would seem to be tactically wise. Michael Whatley, Mike Collins, and Mike Rogers, the Republican Senate nominees in the swing states of North Carolina, Georgia, and Michigan, respectively, all spoke at the convention. So, too, did Jon Husted and Ken Paxton, who are running in Ohio and Texas, two states Trump won by more than 10 points in 2024, but where polls suggest that Democrats have a chance to pick up Senate seats. Even when voters believe that the president is to blame for painful gas-price increases, they may be unsure about how aligned their local House or Senate candidate is with the White House. Heading into the midterms, Trump is going out of his way to make sure no such uncertainty exists.

Who Needs Regulation When Trump Is President?
2026-09-14

Who Needs Regulation When Trump Is President?

The Atlantic | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
One consistent complaint against the Trump administration is that it is imposing personalist rule, in which the whims of the leader override the law. Now Donald Trump has come out forcefully against regulating artificial intelligence—and his argument is based almost explicitly on the superiority of his omniscience over any formal legal process. He is not so much refuting the charge of personalism as turning it into a promise. “The only control or ‘guardrails’ that AI needs is a STRONG AND SMART (High IQ!) PRESIDENT, and the U.S.A. has that, in spades!” he wrote in a self-refuting post on Truth Social. Even the most market-friendly economist will concede that government intervention is required when any private activity creates an “externality”—that is, a cost imposed on people who are not involved in a transaction. A factory dumping toxic waste into a stream has heretofore provided the textbook case of an externality necessitating regulation. But AI bots destroying human civilization—as executives are warning could happen—clearly represents a much costlier externality. This is, in fact, probably the strongest conceivable case for regulation. The intense competitive pressure among AI firms, which have a much stronger incentive to innovate quickly than to ensure their innovations are safe, enhances the case further. Skeptics have valid reasons to worry about poorly designed regulation that either entrenches the current industry leaders or permits Chinese AI to overtake the American version. Those are reasons to demand well-designed regulations, not to avoid any rules at all. Read: The singularity is not what it seems Against this overwhelming rationale, Trump offers, in place of any neutral standards, his characteristic faith in his own prerogatives. Trump tends to discount the need for laws even in areas where he favors government action. He opposed congressional authorization for war with Iran. He has not asked Congress to levy tariffs, preferring instead to raise or lower them and provide carve-outs to affected firms based on his whim. If anyone needs it, the incumbent president’s behavior provides excellent evidence against personalist rule in general and, in the case of AI, specifically. For one thing, his decisions are not necessarily aligned with the public interest. Trump has formed a political alliance with a handful of wealthy men who have a strong personal stake in the development and shape of AI. The dual roles of David Sacks, Trump’s most important AI adviser and an investor in AI, have caused even some White House officials to express concern about conflicts of interest. Additionally, Trump has a long-standing tendency to focus on short-term concerns over long-term ones. Trump’s preference for immediate gratification explains many of his decisions, including defunding scientific and medical research, accelerating fossil-fuel emissions, and denigrating American soft power globally. Pushing forward AI development delivers financial benefits to Trump allies who have plied his party with donations. Trump seems to believe that the AI build-out is driving the economy at the moment. It is difficult to imagine any long-term concern persuading Trump to forgo the short-term benefits of the AI boom. Finally, on the IQ point, Trump is correct that a high level of analytic intelligence is necessary to regulate wisely. This is not, however, a reason to empower Donald Trump. Although he possesses a distinct animal cunning and an uncanny sense for human weakness, Trump isn’t the type to dig in and understand new problems in all of their complexity. He does not appear to read texts of any length or substance, and he is reportedly unable to tolerate briefings of any depth. His public remarks are rambling and error-filled, and rely on a shrinking pool of vocabulary terms. Trump does have expertise in a handful of realms, such as construction, entertainment, and men’s discount shoeware. Artificial intelligence and technology in general are not among his fields of specialization. There are many reasons the United States needs clear, stable regulations on artificial intelligence, rather than relying on the whims of a leader. That is true even if the leader is public-minded and deeply versed in policy intricacies. It is all the more true if the leader is Donald Trump.

Why Does Donald Trump Hate This Sculpture?
2026-09-14

Why Does Donald Trump Hate This Sculpture?

The Atlantic | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
The sculpture that Donald Trump wants removed from the National Mall has a reputation even among its admirers for being a bit inscrutable. Dedicated in 1967 at what is now the National Museum of American History, José de Rivera’s Infinity looks, fittingly, like an infinity symbol with an extra twist. (A bow? A pretzel?) By pairing the technical prowess required to create smooth, curved metal with the delicate spinning of a music box, the slow-turning work seems to take a different form each time you pass it. The museum’s architect, Walker O. Cain, wrote that Infinity “has the characteristic of stimulating everyone who has seen it to create his own personal imaginative interpretation.” But the president does not appear to have been stimulated. “That strange modernist sculpture tells visitors nothing about America, inspires no one, and honors none of our Great American Heroes,” Trump wrote on Truth Social Friday night. Trump insisted that the Smithsonian replace Infinity with a 30-foot high “Colossus” of George Washington. In the same post, he called for the mounting of a five-year exhibition about the Founding Father to open immediately (museum shows of such magnitude typically take years to plan), and said the museum should create a “Center for American Heroes.” Trump also wrote that the institution should add another, 11-foot-tall Washington statue to the museum’s Flag Hall. That one was displayed at the Grand Prix race held in Washington, D.C., this summer as part of Trump’s Freedom 250 festivities. He’d like it dedicated at the museum this Thursday. Trump’s latest demands might be partially understood as a test of how far he can push an institution that he does not control. They came just three days after the Smithsonian’s secretary, Lonnie Bunch, announced his upcoming retirement, following a year and a half of standing between the museum complex and the White House. In a July executive order, the president called on the National Park Service, which is part of the executive branch, to place signs outside the National Museum of American History accusing it of partisan bias. Few would question the appropriateness of having a healthy amount of George Washington in an American-history museum, but Trump’s ask might be a little too redundant. For one thing, a Washington sculpture can already be found on the museum’s second floor. Classical in style and portraying the first president as a kind of Zeus figure, Horatio Greenough’s 1841 statue boasts chiseled muscles that rival even the machismo AI depictions Trump circulates of himself and Washington online. And when approached from the east, Rivera’s Infinity—the aspirational site of Trump’s Washington colossus—elegantly frames another abstract form: the obelisk known as the Washington Monument. Trump’s call for changes to the museum fits a few recent patterns: He has been on a campaign to institute his preferred version of history in the national museum system. He has begun to leave his mark, and occasionally his name, across the architectural landscape of Washington, D.C. And while doing so, he appears to have indicated a distaste for abstract art. Earlier this month, the Kennedy Center, which Trump controls, took down Joel Shapiro’s playful metal sculpture Blue. When the Trump administration—addressing a controversy at the National Portrait Gallery last year—criticized a decidedly representational Amy Sherald painting of a trans woman as Lady Liberty, part of its complaint was that, in the words of the administration, “the Statue of Liberty is not an abstract canvas for political expression.” The use of abstract reads like an insult. Trump has pushed a classical makeover in D.C.. He has issued executive orders mandating classical designs as the default for new federal buildings, for example, and remounted figurative statues that were torn down during the Black Lives Matter movement. He has mused about the White House upgrading its columns to a higher Greek order (adding more classical to the neoclassical); his most prized project, the White House ballroom, looks like a Corinthian-columned department store. In his first executive order on architecture, he said federal buildings should command respect. When it comes to the Rivera outside the National Museum of American History, Trump’s issue with it—and with abstraction largely—might not be that the sculpture “says nothing about America,” but that it doesn’t say what he wants it to. That is, he can’t control what it says at all. The American artist Robert Motherwell wrote in a 1951 essay celebrating abstraction that one of the most striking aspects of the approach is its “nakedness,” or the way it turns, with such decisiveness, away from the symbols we know. “How many rejections on the part of her artists! Whole worlds—the world of objects, the world of power and propaganda, the world of anecdotes, the world of fetishes and ancestor worship,” he wrote. With abstract works such as Infinity, we’re free to find our own reading—or not. Whereas abstraction attempts to reject passed-down meanings, the image of George Washington is layered with them. Part of what is so moving about, say, getting up close to Washington’s military uniform at the National Museum of American History is the way it challenges how we’ve come to see him as an icon rather than as an actual person. Read: A strikingly complex portrait of a Founding Father Abstraction has long been subject to right-wing attacks. During the Red Scare, for instance, George A. Dondero, a U.S. representative from Michigan, crusaded to rid American art of what he saw as communist influence. In a 1949 speech that could be ripped from Truth Social today, he framed a list of 20th-century art movements as un-American weapons for destruction: “Dadaism aims to destroy by ridicule. Expressionism aims to destroy by aping the primitive and insane,” he said. “Abstractionism aims to destroy by the creation of brainstorms.” Sure, the unpredictable responses to abstract art—the brainstorms—can be a little intimidating. How many of us have stood, a bit clueless, before Jackson Pollock’s riotous colors or Cy Twombly’s frenetic scratches and wondered if we might need a Ph.D. for this? But there’s also a certain joy to find in it. One National Museum of American History curator, David Shayt, embraced the compounding, shifting meanings of abstraction in a 2008 essay about the museum’s Rivera. The sculpture marked an early work of abstract art commissioned by the U.S. government, but, Shayt argues, the commission was more about the government’s belief that Rivera, a prominent abstract-expressionist sculptor known for his twisting metal forms, could capture the museum’s dual identity in its early days as the National Museum of History and Technology. Cain, the architect, had convinced the government that the sculpture would be a mechanical model of the solar system, a “twenty-first-century orrery.” But Rivera, who cited the abstractionist Piet Mondrian and the sculptor Constantin Brâncuși as among his influences, asserted no such thing. “What I make represents nothing but itself,” he said. That didn’t stop the National Museum of American History’s staff from making their own meaning over the years, particularly as employees of a “pragmatic museum of history rather than a museum of art,” Shayt wrote. “Is it a mathematical symbol? A wisp of battlefield smoke? A tennis stroke?” he asked of Infinity. One of the sculpture’s greatest attributes, he continued, is “an expressiveness that can evolve in step with the evolution of the museum whose entrance it fronts.” For an example of the kind of aesthetic Trump prefers, you don’t have to go far from the Rivera. A short walk brings you to Freedom Plaza, where the National Park Service has installed a pop-up exhibition for the nation’s 250th birthday. Rows of traditional, metal statues of Revolutionary War soldiers line the plaza—many, notably, represent enslaved individuals. Standing on a high plinth, meanwhile, is Caesar Rodney, a slaveholder and Declaration of Independence signer, who looms over the display. The statue had been removed in Delaware during the Black Lives Matter protests, only to be restored in Washington as some conservatives began championing him as a symbol of the left’s overreach. Trump has placed a similar style of traditional statue—direct, simple—throughout the White House Rose Garden and envisions much of the same for the Garden of American Heroes, another of his planned D.C. projects, where abstraction is explicitly forbidden. There’s very little room for interpretation in these sorts of statues. They mean what they mean. Much of the Trump-era fight over history has boiled down to a struggle about trust. The Trump administration says the history the Smithsonian tells is not patriotic enough; the institution counters that Americans can hold patriotism and complexity at once. The National Museum of American History museum’s director, Anthea Hartig, told a congressional committee this summer: “Telling our history fully is not anti-American. It is a form of respect for the American people.”  Abstraction, too, holds a kind of esteem for its viewers, trusting them to navigate its symbol-less terrain. In his essay, Motherwell wrote that the emergence of abstract art is one sign that there are still people who “know how to respect and follow their inner feelings, no matter how irrational or absurd they may first appear.” On the west side of the museum stands another abstract piece, a massive, 40-foot Alexander Calder that the museum calls a “monument to modernity.” Its jagged black edges reach outward, conveying a sense of rupture or change. On Saturday, the area around the Calder was roped off with caution tape, surely something to do with conservation or work on the museum grounds. But it felt a little ominous, as though it were a warning not for museumgoers, but for the art itself.

No Rolling Power Outages for California Since 2020 - Thanks to 17,000 MW of New Battery Storage

Summer loads typically peak mid-day and follow solar generation closely... batteries help make managing supply and demand easier, but huge capacity isn't necessarily needed or helpful. By contrast, it's winter loads that experience the morning/evening "double peak". That's where these large capacities come into play... the sun directly warms houses during the middle of the day (cutting the need for heating) and the batteries soak up excess dollar generation, which then gets spent as the sun sets and in the morning hours the next day. You're right the article is light on details. They did install 17,000 MW worth of batteries; ~13,500 was grid-scale storage and the rest was residential supported through rebates. The rebates on the batteries you earn over time, and to do so you must export some of your stored battery power back to the grid during peak hours. So about 15-20% was residential people incentivized to use their home batteries as storage and the rest was grid storage. The article doesn't point out that California also added 27,000 MW of generation capacity also but good news it was almost entirely renewables, wind and solar mostly; natural gas power generation has been flat in it's power generation capacity. They still need the NG to help deal with the hourly volatility of the grid, but they didn't need to add any and overall usage is down around 50%. And to your point, the other thing they did in the interim periods is halfway through this (2022) they asked people to voluntarily use less power via these Flex Alerts, sending the population alerts to limit usage. 2023 it was a colder year on average so they didn't have to go that path. So overall it is a pretty good success story. I got this link from a Slashdot poster years ago and have been fascinated with it ever since. https://www.gridstatus.io/live... Yep, curtailment is just future battery storage. It would be stupid to build a bazillion GWh of storage without the generation to fill it. Both are happening at increasing speed. I believe that is actually the normal industry nomenclature practice: rating the storage in power available, not energy stored. I guess the rationale is that the power is usually not needed for a long time. I think the rating is for a specific period, maybe 3 hours.There isn't an industry standard. That's because power storage is used for many different things:1) Grid stabilization for load swings. Timescale = minutes.2) Daily load smoothing. I believe that is actually the normal industry nomenclature practice: rating the storage in power available, not energy stored. I guess the rationale is that the power is usually not needed for a long time. I think the rating is for a specific period, maybe 3 hours. There isn't an industry standard. That's because power storage is used for many different things: 1) Grid stabilization for load swings. Timescale = minutes. 2) Daily load smoothing. They're typically Lithium Iron Phosphate (LFP), good for 5000+ cycles before they degrade enough to be a problem. So charging every day they're good for about 10 to 15 years. In practice, they'll have a site with multiple generations of batteries. So they're always extending their capacity and keeping old batteries in service. Eventually replacing degraded packs with new equipment when it is cheaper (in terms of storage capacity vs cost) to swap out the equipment than to simply add new. Do you think someone will find Grape Ape on the moon with this model? So we have finally reached the point where we need an AI foundation model to tell us what is hiding in the shadows on the Moon. Somewhere, a crater is now worried about being classified. Jokes aside, this is actually one of the more sensible applications of these models. The interesting part is not that it is "AI," but that it combines data from multiple instruments and resolutions. Humans are pretty good at looking at one dataset and finding something interesting. We are considerably less good at mentally registering tens of thousands of observations from nine instruments and noticing that three seemingly unrelated measurements line up over the same patch of lunar real estate. The open dataset may be even more valuable than the model. If researchers can reproduce the results, retrain it, and compare it against conventional geological analysis, we might eventually learn whether the model is actually discovering things or merely becoming extremely good at finding things that look like whatever was in its training data. And if it helps locate lunar ice deposits, that has some pretty obvious practical value. Water on the Moon is not just something to drink; it potentially means oxygen, hydrogen, fuel, and considerably less stuff that has to be launched from Earth. Finding it before we start digging is probably a worthwhile use of compute. Of course, the first scientist to publish "AI discovers giant face on Moon" is going to have some explaining to do. News and Politics - Slate Magazine
Trump Wants to Prevent Future Presidents From Fighting Climate Change
2026-09-14

Trump Wants to Prevent Future Presidents From Fighting Climate Change

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. It’s a great day for people who hate the idea of a livable planet. For everyone else, not so much. The Trump administration today plans to both rescind existing rules on climate pollution from power plants and undercut future administrations’ ability to regulate those emissions. These moves face court challenges. But if they go through, they would gut what’s left of U.S. climate policy and undermine efforts to stave off the worst effects of climate change. What do the new Trump rules actually say? Both the Obama and Biden administrations used the Clean Air Act to try to reduce greenhouse gas emissions, which drive climate change. Now Donald Trump, who has labeled climate change a “con job” and twice pulled out of international agreements meant to mitigate it, is reversing those efforts. One of his new rules would reportedly eliminate Biden-era regulations that required power plants that run on coal and natural gas to limit their carbon emissions. If that rule matches a version reporters obtained last year, it will argue that power plants don’t contribute significantly to pollution or climate change because they’re “a small and decreasing part of global emissions,” even though coal remains the worst-polluting fossil fuel. The second proposal would be even more far-reaching. It’s expected to claim that power plant emissions in general don’t meet the Clean Air Act’s standard for Environmental Protection Agency regulation. The power industry is, behind transportation, the second-largest source of planet-warming emissions in the United States and the second-largest producer of greenhouse gas emissions in the world. Kneecapping the EPA’s ability to regulate the industry under the Clean Air Act would gut one of the main ways Democratic presidents have tried to minimize climate impacts and tie future Democratic presidents’ hands. What else is Trump doing to fuel climate change? Earlier this year, Trump rescinded a long-standing EPA finding that greenhouse gases jeopardize human health. That determination, known as the “endangerment finding,” had been the basis of federal climate regulation for nearly two decades. Without it, the administration has effectively given itself carte blanche to go after all manner of climate regulations. Trump has lifted restrictions on vehicle tailpipe emissions, canceled billions for clean energy projects in blue states, and delayed efforts to eliminate chemicals that are hundreds or thousands of times better at trapping heat than carbon dioxide. How is the administration justifying this? Trump officials argue that climate rules burden the fossil fuel industry, hurt the economy, and increase what Americans pay for electricity. In truth, the EPA’s own assessments recently found that repealing power-plant pollution standards will do little to lower utility bills or save the coal industry, which has been in decline for decades. But it would be a major favor to the GOP’s fossil fuel benefactors, saving the industry billions. The economic impacts for the rest of us will probably be imperceptible, particularly when compared to the damage Trump’s moves threaten to do to the environment and to public health. Surely there’s something that people worried about climate change can do about these new rules? Democratic-led states and environmental organizations will sue over these rules, as they have over the others. Those challenges could take years to resolve. But the Supreme Court seems likely to hear at least some of them, and its conservative majority has shown a blasé attitude toward climate change. In 2016, Chief Justice John Roberts led a behind-the-scenes effort to block the Clean Power Plan, an Obama initiative to slash emissions, in part citing “irreparable harm” to energy companies. In 2022, the court narrowed the EPA’s ability to regulate power plant emissions, forcing Joe Biden to adopt more-piecemeal measures. That doesn’t sound promising. So what would it mean if these rules took effect? Think of the fight against climate change like a tug-of-war, with emissions on one end of the rope and efforts to mitigate them on the other. Trump’s moves would tilt the playing field toward emissions. This means not only larger and faster changes to the climate but also an increase in other health effects due to the pollution that fossil-fuel-burning power plants spew, which can negatively affect anyone with lungs. The Biden administration estimated that its power plant rules would’ve prevented up to 1,200 premature deaths and 360,000 asthma attacks in 2035 alone. Some Democratic-led states have stepped into the breach, pursuing tougher rules of their own and even trying to force fossil fuel companies to pay for damage from wildfires, floods, and other climate-related disasters. Still, states probably can’t balance out a president who’s doing everything in his power to spur climate change along. And time is of the essence. Pondering the potential heat death of humanity is a bummer of a way to start the week, I know. To get your mind off it for the rest of the night, my colleagues suggest: TV worth rewatching: What is it about the HBO miniseries Band of Brothers that keeps viewers coming back for more, 25 years after it originally aired? Slate’s Rebecca Onion investigates why fans are so attached to the show. This game show tradition was Alex Trebek’s favorite: What is Jeopardy!’s annual Teen Tournament? Claire McNear, America’s preeminent Jeopardy! reporter, did a deep dive into the beloved event—which disappeared after Trebek’s death—and got some news about its future. Some snack science: Consumer Reports recently published a scary-sounding investigation on chemicals present in common kids’ snacks. Epidemiologist Gideon Meyerowitz-Katz breaks down the science and concludes that the results are actually pretty reassuring. The Trump Proposal to Pay Moms for Childcare May Sound Like a Good Thing. But There’s a Truly Disturbing Catch.
2026-09-14

The Trump Proposal to Pay Moms for Childcare May Sound Like a Good Thing. But There’s a Truly Disturbing Catch.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. For decades now, American feminists have asked our government to do more to support families and children. We have agitated for paid parental leave, universal childcare, better public schools, child allowances, and other help for struggling parents. Many feminists, especially socialist ones, have demanded wages for housework as well as childcare—salaries, in other words, for mothers who stay home. Now the Trump administration is reportedly doing something along those lines: extending financial support to mothers who choose to care for their children at home rather than put them in daycare. Good news, right? Wrong. The exact details of the plan are not yet official, but the gist has been shared with reporters. And as it turns out, the administration isn’t expanding support to parents at all. The policy will draw from funds that currently largely cover the care of children of low-income single moms. What’s more, the new policy will benefit only mothers who are married. It’s something like an anti-feminist reverse–Robin Hood: Take from poor single mothers to give to more stable ones. In the 1990s, the government created a program to help low-income families afford childcare so that parents could work or go to school. The benefit is fairly modest, averaging about $9,000 per child annually, in a country where childcare costs can exceed $30,000 a year. The overwhelming majority—80 percent—of the recipients of this federal childcare credit are single parents, and the overwhelming majority of them are single mothers. Given the Trump administration’s emphasis on this plan as a boon for stay-at-home mothers, you may wonder if the single moms presently in the workforce could use childcare funds to pay themselves to stay home. The answer is no. If the new policy is implemented, the payment would apply solely to married stay-at-home moms. The single mothers who rely now on the childcare subsidies would have to keep working (i.e., meet the current requirement to get the subsidy) or marry a gainfully employed man. (If only they had thought of that!) Parents who are unmarried by cohabitating also wouldn’t qualify for the stay-at-home benefit. Married mothers, though, could collect a government payment to stay home, so long as their husbands work full time and for pay. (The rule applies to parents, but the vast majority of stay-at-home parents in America are stay-at-home moms, and conservatives are explicit to state that they believe it’s best if mothers stay home and men work for pay.) Conservatives might argue that the government has no business paying single mothers to stay home. And fair enough—but then it shouldn’t be paying married ones to stay home either. And, worse, the administration is contemplating expanding funding eligibility without actually increasing the amount of money up for grabs. That would mean that there would be “more parents competing for the same dollars,” as one fellow at a conservative think tank put it. As a result, some money that is currently earmarked largely for poor single moms will end up going to mothers with working husbands who choose to stay home. This initiative is transparently not about helping all children to have a parent at home if that is what their parents desire. If it were, then it would apply to all lower-income families equally, regardless of whether a breadwinner spouse is in the picture. This is the government paying only for married moms to stay home. Single moms apparently don’t deserve that luxury. This is especially galling given that conservatives correctly point out that children of married couples tend to do better than children of single parents. Children raised in two-parent families in which one parent is employed full time already have a host of advantages, while children raised in low-income single-parent households are more likely to face significant challenges. Why in the world would we take from the least privileged to give to the group conservatives themselves say are the best off? The only explanation is that this isn’t really about supporting children. It’s about rewarding some mothers and punishing others. This is all part of a broader conservative effort to push women out of the workforce and back into the home, as well as into traditional family structures. Earlier this week, Katie Miller (wife of White House deputy chief of staff for policy Stephen Miller) lamented how “terrible” it is that women now outnumber men in the workforce—a comment she made on The Katie Miller Podcast, which is her job. In the Heritage Foundation proposal, titled “Saving America by Saving the Family,” that seems to be the blueprint for this proposed Trump policy, the group argues: “In promoting new family policies, one must be mindful of mistakes of the past. For example, credits designed specifically to benefit poor single mothers may be well intended, but they have proven to incentivize single motherhood in poor communities and trap women there through marriage penalties.” The result, according to the proposal, is that “the growth of the federal government displaced men from their traditional role as providers. Welfare moved the primary responsibility to provide for a family from fathers to the government. … The expansion of welfare programs, particularly AFDC, made the federal government the de facto husband for millions of poor women across the country.” The implication is that the federal government should stop supporting poor single moms so that they might hitch themselves to a man, and that the federal government should financially reward women who follow the traditional script. The Heritage Foundation is particularly explicit in this aim. Its version of a policy that would support stay-at-home parents would actually impose income minimums for a household to qualify—in other words, the support would go not to the poorest families but to those who are more stable. Heritage proposes tax breaks that would apply only to two-parent married families with one stay-at-home spouse, and that would offer even more generous payments when those families have multiple children. It also proposes special bank accounts exclusively for people who marry young (before the age of 30). And Heritage is explicit that this shift in resources (which also involves ending the Head Start program) is part of a broader right-wing project that includes attacks on contraception, abortion, in vitro fertilization, and LGBTQ+ rights. From “Saving America by Saving the Family”: “A large share of the blame for the present malady lies in moral and cultural trends that have both fed and been fed by government policy. Chief among these is surely the sexual revolution, which separated the sex act from marriage and childbearing. It is not just that sexual mores were revolutionized. Technology and policy also played key supporting roles. Starting in the 1960s, the Pill and other contraceptives swept the country—which promised to reduce the unwanted consequences of casual sex with multiple partners across a lifetime.” The Heritage report points a finger at no-fault divorce, child protection laws, and government secularism as further causes of our culture’s alleged decline. And it really zeros in on women going to college: “Social pressures, from popular culture to guidance counselors to parents, now encourage almost everyone to go to college and delay getting married and having children,” the report states, before spotlighting a study that found that “women who complete 4 years of college are less likely to have a child, while completion rates of 4 years of college rose 10 percent for women over the past decade.” The connection is clear: Conservatives want more women to marry and have babies; women going to college is an impediment to that. If we’re giving parents money for childcare, it does indeed seem fair to allow that money to support at-home care too, whether the carer is the child’s parent or a nanny. The French system does something like this. It offers paid parental leave, then a variety of care options afterward, largely cost-adjusted according to family income. Parents who wish to stay home may qualify for some cash; parents who want to hire a nanny may qualify for a subsidized rate; parents who want to send their children to France’s much-beloved crèches can do that too, paying an amount that varies according to what they can afford. If “pro-family” conservatives were pushing this kind of system, I promise you feminists would cheer—even those, like me, who think it’s generally a good thing when women work. But that isn’t what this Trump policy does. This policy is essentially a financial reward system for married stay-at-home mothers, funded at the expense of poor single moms. It’s punishing the children of women whose lives don’t align with the right-wing feminine ideal. And it is part of a larger agenda to push women out of school, out of the workforce, into early motherhood, and into the home—whether we want to be there or not. Democrats Are Laughing at Trump’s Midterm Plan. They Shouldn’t.
2026-09-12

Democrats Are Laughing at Trump’s Midterm Plan. They Shouldn’t.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-12

Visit Original Website
Welcome to the Surge, your weekly political sanity check: What matters, what might, and what’s just political theater. Sign up here to get it in your inbox. Welcome to this week’s historic edition of the Surge, here in our brand-new home. Do you like the imported marble floors and silk window treatments? We’ll tell ya, it was a real hassle getting those through the Strait of Hormuz. Indeed, today is the exciting launch of Surge 2.0. To recap, each Saturday we’ll break down the biggest news of the week into three categories: what mattered most, what we’re keeping an eye on, and what’s unfathomably inane. Plus, there’s a fresh new section of quick hits at the end. Let us know what you think! Now we begin with the GOP’s counterprogramming to the kickoff of the new NFL season. Republicans this week held their midterm convention, something that we “definitely” “watched.” Aside from a few stray clips and a couple of other newsy items we’ll deal with below, though, the most enduring message from the event were these words from President Donald Trump: “I’m asking you to pretend that I’m on the ballot. I’m on the ballot.” He would also tell the attendees, during a mock pledge, that they will “go to hell” if they don’t vote. Democrats have celebrated this unsurprising decision of Trump’s to put himself forthrightly in the center of the midterm discussion. It’s a move that neatly aligns with Democrats’ strategy of making the midterms a referendum on Trump, whose approval rating is atrocious. But we’re not willing to say that Trump’s strategy is all downside for Republicans. The Republicans were never going to be able to stuff the president in a box this election season. Consider, for example, that Trump forced the Republican National Committee to hold its first-ever midterm convention in order to celebrate him. And at this point, the thing that would worsen these midterms from bad to catastrophic for the GOP is if MAGA stayed home. Poll after poll indicates that Republicans are suffering from an enthusiasm gap. Democrats and Democratic-leaning independents don’t have to be told that Trump is on the ballot this year; it’s Republicans who could use all the motivational nudges they can get. Bringing MAGA enthusiasm closer to what Democrats are showing wouldn’t just help Republicans save, say, their Senate seats in places like Alaska, Ohio, Texas, and Iowa either. Sen. Susan Collins needs MAGA voters to turn out in rural Maine just as much as Ken Paxton needs them to in Texas. Although Republican strategists would prefer a more targeted deployment of Trump’s motivational tools to specific races where his presence would help, the president’s never really been a scalpel guy. He’s foreclosing on more-delicate persuasion efforts and pushing the one button available to him—let’s get my people out there in force—as hard as he can. There’s no choice but to roll with it. Developers of artificial intelligence have long been undersocialized creeps who openly and casually talk about how the technology they are creating has a decent chance of ending all human life. This week, a couple such comments went viral. Jacob Coxon, who has worked at both OpenAI and Anthropic, resigned from the latter and said that “neither company is acting responsibly,” asserting that the “people building AI earnestly believe that it could kill us all by the end of the decade.” His thread on X has hundreds of thousands of reposts. Another Anthropic employee, Evan Hubinger, agreed with Coxon, adding, “We really do earnestly believe AI could kill all humans!” (That exclamation point will haunt our dreams.) “I personally think it is >10% within the next decade.” While the public debates whether these fears are overblown—only the robots know for sure—the conversation seems to have reached people who might have the power to do something about it. Democrats in both the House and the Senate on Tuesday discussed establishing select committees on artificial intelligence. (Even if Democrats win one or both chambers, this will be a challenge, as various committees with jurisdiction over A.I. won’t want to cede power. This is one reason why the machines deserve to win.) And even some Republicans saw where the wind was blowing. Ted Cruz, who chairs the Senate Commerce Committee, noted that “AI is advancing at an extraordinary pace, and some of the risks are dangerous and frightening” and stated that he’s working on legislation “to address catastrophic risks involving biological or nuclear threats.” Our own A.I. policy preference is that the government should unplug it, then repurpose gigantic data centers into the biggest Chili’s franchises you’ve ever seen. We’re hoping that the public, which was never consulted on whether it wanted to reorganize life this way, is at least dragging politicians somewhat in our direction. Cruz, for example, hasn’t become an A.I. opponent, by any means, but his remarks were a notable tone shift from his substantial work this Congress trying to limit states’ ability to control the technology. The scope and terms of a federal A.I. regulation bill will be one of the biggest debates, in both party primaries, in the 2028 presidential election. At the GOP midterm convention, Trump promised $5,000 “divided” checks to every American adult if Republicans hold both the House and Senate in the midterms. (This came as a surprise to House and Senate Republicans.) It’s an act of political desperation and objectively incorrect as policy. There’s no trove of tariff lucre from which to pay “dividends.” Deficits are soaring, and tossing another $1 trillion–plus of stimulus into this economy would send inflation and interest rates rocketing further upward. The bond market could not be clearer about this. This goes against everything congressional Republicans believe about fiscal policy regardless of the economic environment, and congressional Democrats would have little interest in facilitating Trump’s bribe. But Trump has made Republicans go along with terrible ideas before—for example, his political career—and it would be uncomfortable for Democrats to uniformly band together to oppose people getting cash. So can we guarantee that this pledge will have no political pull, or that it couldn’t pass Congress? No. Mothers, don’t let your sons grow up to be populists. www.theregister.com - Articles
Microsoft drafts feel-good AI model guidelines and wants your input
2026-09-15

Microsoft drafts feel-good AI model guidelines and wants your input

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Redmond outlines 'aspirational' goals for model behavior, but gives itself a pass if it gets things wrong Microsoft on Monday debuted draft principles for governing how the company will train future AI models, as part of a public consultation process. The Windows biz has solicited feedback to refine its Humanist AI Code of Conduct [PDF], with the expectation of applying the guidelines to model development next year. "This Code of Conduct is motivated by a single overriding objective: that humans must retain meaningful control over AI so that it can help people live healthier, happier, and more productive lives," the company explained in its bid for public feedback. "It is the primary governing document informing how we train MAI models, the technical controls, the operational and monitoring systems we implement, and the organizational culture that underpins all of this." The focus is on Microsoft AI (MAI) models as a source of health, happiness, and productivity, and not on other potential sources of well-being, like employment. In July, Microsoft announced the layoff of ~4,800 employees, roles that EVP and chief people officer Amy Coleman insisted "are not being replaced by AI." That followed another roughly 875 layoffs at LinkedIn in May. The 38-page document makes appealing declarations, such as insisting Microsoft's MAI models won't misrepresent their reasoning, conceal their capabilities, or pursue their own goals – behaviors other AI models have happily done. It is intended to govern future MAI models as they (perhaps) approach "superintelligence" over the next decade. Microsoft defines that term to mean "AI systems that are more intelligent and capable than all humans combined." That definition falls short because it leaves "intelligence" undefined. There's a significant gap between "intelligence" as a high score on a given benchmark and "intelligence" as the capacity to navigate the world and effect change within a society of individuals who may have different agendas. The fear is Skynet, but the reality is still Roomba. If AI manages to solve a problem that humans believe is impossible based on our current knowledge of physics – say, devising warp drive to travel faster than the speed of light – then the case for "superintelligence" would be unassailable. But brute force computation and exploration of knowledge captured in training data don't qualify. Microsoft's current model lineup includes MAI-Transcribe-2, MAI-Thinking-1, MAI-Code-1.1-Flash, MAI-Image-2.6, MAI-Voice-2, and MAI-Cyber-1-Flash. Its general-purpose MAI models do not currently rank among the most "intelligent" models at the moment. So it's unclear whether Redmond's future models will be capable enough to generate hand-wringing that has kept Anthropic's Mythos and Fable models, and OpenAI's Astra model, in the public eye. Nonetheless, Microsoft has timed its human primacy manifesto well, given how Anthropic and OpenAI have called for sufficiently acceptable regulatory intervention amid their unwillingness to practice self-restraint and their evident inability to keep tabs on what their models have been doing to third-party websites. The current US administration doesn't appear to want the job of regulating AI, so Microsoft can more or less write its own ticket. And it could do so without any consequence. Unlike the Microsoft Enterprise AI Services Code of Conduct, which defines how customers are allowed to use company AI services and threatens loss of product access for rule violations, the Humanist AI Code of Conduct has no teeth. It's designed to cover Microsoft employees and perhaps contractors – the ones training MAI models – and not customers. But anyone anticipating internal tribunals to hold company AI researchers accountable for failing to catch any of the enumerated sins, like improper data handling, should be aware that Microsoft has framed its document as articulating goals, not setting a standard to which it will hold itself. On page 25, the company says, "The Code of Conduct is both descriptive and aspirational. It outlines what we are working toward and is not a complete account of current model behavior. We acknowledge a gap between trained defaults today and the complete future scope of Humanist AI. "This document should therefore be read as a north star. Both for our development and training. It is not a guarantee of present-day performance." A code of conduct without stated consequences for breaking its rules risks being as empty as Google's abandoned motto of yore, "Don't be evil." So far, it looks like a public relations exercise.
